Constitutional Reform acts
Regulating Act of 1773:
* Ended dual system of bengal of clive
* First step to regulate/ centralisation of the East India Company's administration. Required the Company to report revenue and civil matters to the British government.
* Designated the Governor of Bengal as the Governor-General of Bengal (Warren Hastings was the first).
* Established a Supreme Court of Judicature at Fort William, Calcutta.
* Prohibited the Company’s servants from engaging in private trade.
Pitt's India Act of 1784:
* Created dual control: Distinguished between commercial and political functions of the Company. The company controlled commerce while the British government controlled political affairs.
   * Established the Board of Control to oversee the Company’s civil, military, and revenue matters.
* Territories will be called British possessions
* Reduced the powers of the Governor-General but made him more accountable to the British government.
Charter act 1793
* Renewed company’s commercial privileges for 20 years
* Board of control (home govt) to be paid from Indian revenues till 1919 act (126 years)
* Separate revenue and judicial functions (art 50 same)
Charter Act of 1813:
* For the first time, “explicitly defined” constitutional position of British territories in India and crown’s sovereignty- Company to retain possessions of territories and revenue for 20 years
* Free trade movt- Ended the East India Company's trade monopoly except for tea and trade with China.
* Allowed Christian missionaries to propagate their religion in India.
* Allocated ₹1 lakh annually for the promotion of education in India.
Charter Act of 1833:
* Lease extended by 20 more years.
* Complete centralisation- in hands of governor general
* Ended the Company's monopoly completely, turning it into an administrative body.
   * As per the Act, no Indian citizen was to be denied employment under the Company on the basis of religion, colour, birth, descent, etc.
* Governor-General of Bengal became the Governor-General of India (Lord William Bentinck was the first).
* First step towards codification of laws with a Law Commission under Lord Macaulay.
   * Prohibited slavery in British India.
Charter Act of 1853:
* Introduced open competitive exams for Indian Civil Services (ICS).
* Separated legislative and executive functions of the Governor-General's Council.
   * Created a separate Indian Legislative Council for the first time.
GoI Act 1858:
* Transferred the control of India from the East India Company to the British Crown, establishing direct British rule.
* Abolished the EIC and established the office of the Viceroy of India. Ended the practice of appointing Company officials to high administrative posts, replacing them with Crown appointees.
* Introduced a Secretary of State for India, who was a member of the British Cabinet, to oversee Indian affairs.
   * Created a 15 member Council of India to assist the Secretary of State.
Indian Councils Act of 1861: (POLI)
* Initiated the Portfolio system where council members headed diff departments.
* Gave the Viceroy power to issue Ordinances in emergencies. (After revolt of 1857 fears)
* Created imperial and provincial legislative councils.
* Introduced Indian representation in the legislative council (nominated)- 3 indians nominated- benaras, patiala, sir dinkar rao
Indian Councils Act of 1892:
* Introduced indirect elections for the legislative councils (election was omitted).
* Allowed councils to discuss budget but without voting rights.
* Permitted questioning of the executive on public matters.
Indian Councils Act of 1909 (Morley- Minto Reforms):
* Introduced separate electorates for Muslims.
* Allowed Indians to be “elected” to legislative councils.
* First Indian (Satyendra Prasad Sinha) appointed to the Viceroy’s Executive Council.
Government of India Act 1919 (Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms):
* Introduced Dyarchy (dual governance) in provinces, dividing subjects into Reserved subjects (administered by the Governor and his Executive Council, including Police, press, justice, land revenue, irrigation, famine relief, and forests) and Transferred subjects (administered by Indian Ministers responsible to the legislature, including local self-government, agriculture, public works, education, public health, sanitation, and excise).
* Separated provincial and central budget for the first time
* Introduced bicameralism (Council of State and Legislative Assembly) in the centre.
* Established Public Service Commissions.
* Direct election and franchise on basis of property tax and education
* Extended limited franchise (14%) based on property, education, and taxation. Voting rights to women. 
* Separate electorates to Sikhs, Europeans, Anglo-Indians, and Indian Christians. 
* High commissioner for India appointed in London to look after Indian trade
Government of India Act 1935:
* Introduced Provincial Autonomy, giving elected provincial governments full control over transferred subjects- while reserved subjects remained under the control of the British-appointed Governor. (Partial Autonomy)
* Proposed a federal structure, though never fully implemented.
* Established the Federal Court of India.
* RBI- Royal Commission on Indian Currency and Finance (aka Hilton Young Commission)
* Expanded separate electorates to more communities (SC, labour, women).
* Separated Burma from British India.
Indian Independence Act 1947:
* Declared India and Pakistan as independent sovereign states.
* Partitioned British India into two dominions.
* Ended British rule and transferred power to Constituent Assemblies.
* Marked the formal end of British sovereignty in India.
2. Making the constitution
* First Idea of constituent assembly  by MN Roy in 1934 (He founded the radical democratic party. Swaraj party later mooted in 1935) . Pioneer of CPI. 
* Accepted in principle- by the British in 1940 in August offer.
* Formed in 1946 based on CABINET MISSION PLAN

Composition of Constituent Assembly.- Plan by British cabinet committee 
* Rejection of pak formation 
* Election to Constituent Assembly July- August 1946 for 296 seats( indian + pakistani both);  93 nominated from princely states- no election for them. Total = 389
* Proportional seat to each province and princely state on the basis of Population- one seat per million population.
*  Partially elected and partly nominated.
   * Seat to each British province divided on the basis of separate electorate among Hindu, Muslims, Sikhs proportionally, indirect elections (PR-STV), limited franchise.
   * Representative of princely states to be nominated by head of princely state
* The 15 women members of the Constituent Assembly of India were Rajkumari Amrit Kaur, Sarojini Naidu, Sucheta Kriplani, Vijaya Lakshmi Pandit, and Annie Mascarene, Leela Roy, Renuka Ray, Kamla Chaudhry, Malati Choudhury,  Durgabai Deshmukh, Purnima Banerjee, Ammu Swaminathan, Dakshayani Velayudhan, Begum Aizaz Rasul, Hansa Jivraj Mehta; notably, Lakshmi Sahgal was not among them
* Common centre of Interim govt to control defense, communication, external affairs.
* Communal ques in Central legislature = decided by Simple majority of both community present and voting.
* Post first gen election, province = free to come out of group or call for reconsideration, after 10 years. 
 In addition to making of the Constitution and enacting ordinary laws, the Constituent 
Assembly also performed the following functions: 
o It adopted the National Flag on July 22, 1947. 
o It ratified India's membership Of the Commonwealth in May 1949. 
o It adopted the National Anthem on January 24, 1950. 
o It adopted the National Song on January 24, 1950. 
o It elected Dr. Rajendra Prasad as the first President of India on January 24, 
1950. 
Drafting Committee: Drafting Committee was set up on August 29, 1947. It was 
entrusted with the task of preparing a draft of the new Constitution.  

* The dominant Voice
   * JL Nehru- moved objective resolution (Dec 13, 1946), passed  on Jan 22, 1947) and gave the idea of the present day flag.

*  Changes by Independence Act.
   * Power to frame any constitution; abrogates, alter any British law.
   * Assembly now= legislative body, functions= making constitution enacting ordinary laws. Thus first Parliament of India - Dominion Legislature
   * Muslim league members withdraw, total strength 299 from 389
   *  Constitution enactments
   * 11 sessions over 3 years minus 13 days. Gone through 60 countries' constitutions. 1st Draft considered for 114 days.
   * First reading- 4 November 1948. First Draft by B. R Ambedkar
   * Second reading- 15 Nov, 1948
   * Third reading- 14 November 1949. 
   * Motion on draft constitution passed, Adopted and Enacted. on November 26, 1949.
   * Originally contains preamble 395 articles, 8 schedules. (now 465 articles)
   * Enforcement of constitution -
   * Following provision came into force on November 26, 1949.
   1. Citizenship: Articles 5, 6, 7, 8, 9
   2. President: Article 60 (Oath) and Article 380 (Provisional President)
   3. Elections: Article 324
   4. Parliament: Articles 379 and 388 (Provisional Parliament)
   5. Technical/Transitional: Articles 366, 367, 391, 392
   6. Short Title & Commencement: Articles 393 and 394
   * Remaining provisions on January 26, 1950.


Major Debates for constitution

The Constitution emerged through compromise, balancing opposing views, with universal adult franchise as the sole consensus, uniquely granting secularism via rights to religion, culture, and education.
   * Centralism: B. R. Ambedkar, Jawaharlal Nehru vs Federalism K. Santhanam, K. M. Munshi
   * Parliamentary: B. R. Ambedkar, Jawaharlal Nehru, B. N. Rau vs Presidential: H. V. Kamath, K. T. Shah
   * Fundamental Rights: Sardar Patel, K. M. Munshi, B. R. Ambedkar  vs Directive Principles: B. N. Rau, Alladi Krishnaswamy Iyer
   * Hindi as National Language: Purushottam Das Tandon vs English for Continuity: T. T. Krishnamachari
   * For SC/ST Reservations: B. R. Ambedkar vs Against Separate Electorates: Sardar Patel, Rajendra Prasad
   * Explicitly Secular State: Nehru, Ambedkar, K. M. Munshi vs Religious Freedom with National Unity: Sardar Patel
   * Favored UCC: B. R. Ambedkar vs Opposed (Religious Freedom): Naziruddin Ahmad, Mahboob Ali Baig
   * Socialist Approach: Jawaharlal Nehru, K. T. Shah vs State Control (Not Full Socialism): B. R. Ambedkar
Salient features of Constitution 
   * A nation is a group of people with a shared culture or identity, a state is a political entity with defined borders and sovereignty, and a government is the system or body that runs the state.
   * Resolution of FR & eco changes @ Karachi ssn 1931
Imp features
   * Ppl's Fundamental Identity
   * Authoritative constraints ( ex- +ve Liberty, not -ve in preamble )
   * Impassable Fundamental values => moral ID ( PYQ - Constnl morality)
   * Also political identity ( as ppl can vote due to Universal adult suffrage)
   * Taken from Multiple sources
   * Structural part = GoI act 1935, 
   * Philosophical part = FR, DPSP etc
   * Political part = ( exec, judiciary, leg ) = British govt

Country
	Borrowed Feature(s)
	GOI 1935
	Federal scheme, Public service commission, Judiciary, Emergency provisions, Office of governor
	United Kingdom
	Parliamentary system, Cabinet system, Legislative procedure, Parliamentary privileges, Bicameralism, Rule of Law, Single citizenship, Prerogative writs, nominal role of President
	United States
	Fundamental Rights, Independence of judiciary, Judicial review, Impeachment of the President, Removal of Supreme Court and High Court judges, Office of President and Vice-President
	Ireland
	DPSP, Election of President, Nomination of Rajya Sabha members
	Canada
	Federation with a strong center, Residuary powers with the center, Appointment of Governors by the Center, Advisory jurisdiction of the Supreme Court
	Australia
	Concurrent List, Joint sitting of Parliament, Freedom of trade and commerce
	Germany 
	Emergency provisions, Suspension of Fundamental Rights during Emergency
	(USSR)
	Fundamental Duties, Five-Year Plans
	France
	Ideals of Liberty, Equality, and Fraternity (Preamble); Republic
	South Africa
	Procedure for constitutional amendments, Election of Rajya Sabha members
	Japan
	Procedure established by law, Procedure for law-making
	

      * Mix of parliamentary sovereignty (british) and judicial supremacy (US)- 
      * India has constitutional sovereignty- Checks & balances
      * Parliamentary Sovereignty = ultimate power of decision in people. Limitations- Interpretation power of judiciary, judicial review, Article 142 (complete justice).
      * Judicial Review= Narrow (procedure estd by law); Article 368 (CAA); Removal of Judges by Parliament.
      * Disturbances in checks and balances by  42nd CAA, restoration = 44th CAA
      * Kesavananda Bharati case= judicial review gained
      * Blend of Rigidity and Flexibility-  According to Ambedkar, the constitution is not a legal document, it is a vehicle of change, its spirit is always the spirit of age. Hence, its a written constitution (Art 368) vs CAA with simple majority.
      * Quasi Federalism- term federation is not mentioned. Art 1 describes India as Union of states: Which implies 2 things, one, Indian federation is not the result of an agreement by the states and two, no state has the right to secede from federation
      * Parliamentary/ Responsible government
      * Presence of nominal and real executive (Leadership of PM/ CM)
      * Majority party rule
      * Collective Responsibility of Council of minister to Lok Sabha 
      * Dissolution of LS/ SLA
      * Membership of ministers in legislature
      * Integrated & independent judiciary
      * Separation of power 
      * Philosophy of constitution
      * Pol philosophy= (Preamble, FRs, DPSPs) guides constitutional morality. enabling judicial arbitration. These interpretations and ideals help check executive-legislative overreach (eg. Bulldozer guidelines, NJAC judgement)
      * Moral vision of constitution (along with historical inspiration)
      * Individual freedom and political rights
      * Fundamental Rights. 
      * Universal adult franchise (61st CAA age 18)
      * Social & economic justice
      * Unity in diversity- Asymmetric federalism. A. 371 , 5/6 sch + minority rights
      * DPSPs, FDs
      * Fraternity, Secularism = mutual exclusion of state & religion
      * National unity and integrity 
      * Reasonable restrictions on FRs.
      * Federalism -  competitive, cooperative, collaborative.
      * Independent bodies (CAG,UPSC,EC) 
      * Apart 3 branches ( judiciary, executive, Legislature
Conclusion- Longevity of constitution.. France = 5 changes … Soviet union = 4 in 74 years

4. Preamble
         *   Ingredients
            1. Source of Authority- people of india  
            2. State nature - SSSDR
            3. Obj of constitution- JLEF
            4. Date of adoption/ enactment = 26/11/1949
            * Status on this day = Sovereign Democratic Republic
            * Constitution day = to promote constitutional values among citizens
            * Keywords
            1. Sovereign
            * No external state authority- Freely conduct internal, external affairs (People are the source of authority)
            * Any org membership = not limitation
            * Can acquire/ cede territory ( Berubari union case )
            * Amid problems of the 21st century such as climate change, cybercrime, fin crises that crisscross borders, concepts of sovereignty & territorial integrity played out clearly.
            2. Socialist
            * The 42nd CAA (1976) added "Socialist" to the Preamble, earlier reflected only in DPSPs. 
            * India follows Democratic Socialism (mixed economy) over State Socialism (communism)
            * blending Gandhian statelessness with Marxist classless ideals, but NEP (1991) diluted it through liberalization.
            3. Secular
            * 42nd CAA; Articles 25-28. 
            * Positive concept of Secularism ( all religions in country has same status & support from state)
            4. Democracy
            * Doctrine of popular sovereignty. 
            * Representative parliamentary democracy (UAF, periodic elections)
            * Ambedkar stressed that political democracy cannot last until there lies at the base of it Social democracy (trinity of equality, Liberty & fraternity) = w/o Eq= Supremacy of few, Anarchy; w/o liberty- killing individual initiative. 
            * Types
            1. Direct -Ex- Switzerland. Ppl decide thru
            1. Recall - voter remove representative before expiry
            2. Initiative - ppl propose bill
            3. A referendum is typically a binding vote to approve or reject specific legislation or constitutional changes. 
            4. A plebiscite is generally an advisory, non-binding vote, often used to gauge public opinion on sovereignty, territory, or political status. Ex: Junagadh
            2. Indirect - Ex india
            5. Republic
            * Elected head ( indirectly elected )
            * Pol sovereignty lies in ppl, not single indiv like in UK 
            * No privilege class. pub offices open, no discrimination
            6. Justice
            * Socio- absence of privileges
            * Eco- reduced inequalities and distributive justice
            * Pol- UAF
            * Idea from Russian Revolution
            7. Liberty
            * Def = absence of restraints -> dev opportunity 4 personality growth
            * +ve liberty = "freedom to"
            1. Indiv = free only in society
            2. Engender society for indiv dev
            3. Ex- Freedom of speech, Eco opportunity , pol participation
            * -ve liberty = "freedom from"
            1. Inviolable space of indiv , doesn’t need society
            2. Can do, be whatever want
            3. Ex- Art 19 (Shreya singhal case)
            8. Equality
            * Absence of Class based privileges
            * Adequate opportunity 
            * Civic equality = A 14-18
            * Pol equality
            1. Universal adult suffrage ( Art, 326 )
            1. Article 325- no person is ineligible  to be included in electoral rolls on grounds of religion, race, caste, sex 
            2. No ineligibility - race, rlgn, cast, sex ( A. 320- UPSC )
            9. Fraternity
            * Brotherhood ( single citizenship )
            * FDs- Article 51 A
            * Assure -
            1. Individual dignity ( material betterment )
            2. National integrity  ( A.1 , states - no right to secede )
            * SIGNIFICANCE OF PREAMBLE
            1. KM Munshi = Political horoscope (SDR)
            2. Sir Alladi K. Iyer- Preamble express what we thought and dreamt for so long ( Iyer bhoot - dream )
            3. Pt Thakur Das Bhargava- Key to soul to constitution
            4. Sir Ernest Baker  - Keynote to the constitution contributing political wisdom.
            5. NA Palkhivala- identity card
            * Preamble As part of constn
            1. Berubari union case
            * Preamble = purpose + key to makers' mind
            * not a part of constitution
            2. Kesavnanda Bharati case -Preamble is an integral part of the Constitution but is not enforceable by law + amenable under A368
            3. LIC of india case 1995 -Yes
            * Hence part of constitution but has no legal effect independently  of other part
            * Amendability
            1. Ques 1st in Kesavananda case -
            * Basic element cannot be amended
            2. Only once amended 42nd CAA- SIS (secular, socialist, integrity )
            * Other facts
            1. Non- justiciable
            2. Neither source of power, nor restriction - legislative power
            3. Enacted after the constitution. Hence its a preface but NOT A GUIDE TO CONSTITUTION.
            4. Term, idea technically = USA
5. Union and its territories
            * Art 1- india , ie bharat = union of states, not federation. 
            * Concept taken from = British North American Act (canadian constitution)
            * Territory types- UTs; States; Acquired territories
            * States= no right to secede - India is a nation "holding together"
            * Art 2 parliament = power admit + estb new states that were not previously part of India.
            * No state role
            * Art 3 - Change in state or UTs, internal re-adjustment ( ex-creation of telangana)
            * Increase/ decrease area 
            * Uniting 2 or more states
            * 18th CAA = amended Art 3 - incl UT in definition of 'state' + empower Parliament to form new state or UT by uniting part of any state or UT. Ex: Ladakh was formed with a simple majority in Parliament under Article 3, after the J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019.
            * Prez recommendation for bill
            * State's view reqd ( no impact )
            * ✅ If ceding territory → Requires a constitutional amendment (Art 368) (Berubari union case -100th CAA)
            * ✅ If annexing territory, no constitutional amendment is needed; executive action suffices. 
            * Art 4- Such laws = amendment but not under A. 368


Evolution of states.
After the drama of princely States, Holford state classifications-part A, B, C, D.
            * Part A - erstwhile governors provinces.
            * Party B- Erstwhile princely states with legislature
            * Part C- Erstwhile Chief Commissioners provinces (Ajmer, coorg,cooch behar) with some princely states.
            * Part D- a&N island.

 Chief Commissioner’s states were directly administered by centrally appointed officials with limited autonomy, while Governor’s states had more developed administrative structures with a Governor and often a legislative council.

SK Dhar commission and JVP committee
            * SK dhar = 1948- constituent assembly appointed. The Commission suggested administrative ease rather Linguistic factor.
            * JVP committee (dec 1948) - Jawaharlal Nehru, Vallabhbhai Patel, and Pattabhi Sitaramayya. Formally rejected language as a basis for  re- organization.
            * Linguistic identity got momentum as seen in movements for Aikya Kerala, Samyukta MH & Vishalandhra
            * Forced to create first linguistic state, 1953 Andhra, separating, Telugu speaking area from Madras, followed by date of popular vegetation and death of Potti Sriramulu  (visalandhra movement)
 
Fazl Ali Commission or state reorg commission ( SRC ) 1953
            * Creation of Andhra intensified demand from other regions, forcing to from 3 member state reorganisation Commission-  chairman Justice Fazl Ali, pannikar & Hridaynath Kunzru
            * Accepted language as basis of reorganization. 
            * But Rejected theory of one language, one state. 
            * Unity of India= primary consideration in retaining the country's political unity.
            * 4 factors in any scheme of reorganization.
            * Preservation, strengthening- unity, security of country (Security)
            * linguistic and cultural homogeneity (Culture and Language)
            * Financial, economic and administrative consideration. (Economy)
            * Planning, promotion of people welfare in state. (Social)
            * Suggested abolition of 4 fold classifications under original constitution and creation of 16 states, 3 administered territory.
            * Gov't accepted with minor modifications. 14 states created November 1, 1956. (7th caa)

6. Citizenship
Aspect
	Details
	Meaning & Significance
	Citizenship grants full rights and privileges. Aliens (friendly/enemy) have restricted rights. Enemy aliens can be detained/arrested without protection.
	Rights Exclusive to Citizens
	- Fundamental Rights: Articles 15, 16, 19, 29, 30. 
- Political Rights: Right to vote, contest elections (Parliament), hold public offices (e.g., President, Governor, PM, etc.). 
- Eligibility for President: Naturalized + Born citizens in India eligible. In the USA, only birth citizens can become President.
	Constitutional Provisions
	- Articles 5-11 (Part 2): Define citizenship provisions. 
- Parliament's Authority: Empowered to regulate citizenship through laws.
	Key Citizenship Articles
	- Article 5-8: Defines four categories of citizens at the commencement of the Constitution (26 Jan 1950). 
- Article 9: Indian citizenship lost if a person voluntarily acquired foreign citizenship. 
- Article 10: Citizenship is subject to laws made by Parliament. 
- Article 11: Parlia has the power to regulate acquisition, termination, and other citizenship matters.
	Citizenship Act, 1955
	- Governs citizenship acquisition, termination, and supplementary provisions. 
- Amended 6 times (originally included Commonwealth citizenship, repealed in 2003). 
- ‘Domicile’ is an important criterion mentioned in consti  for citizenship but is not defined in the Constitution.
- 5 ways to get citizenship; 3 ways to lose citizenship
            * Key Amendments to the Citizenship Act, 1955 (6 so far)
            * (i) CAA, 1986- The original 1955 Act gave citizenship to anyone born in India. The 1986 change ensured that children born to foreigners in India did not automatically get citizenship unless one parent was already an Indian citizen.
            * (ii)1992
            * (iii) CAA 2003= Introduced the concept of OCI.
            * (iv) CAA, 2005- nothing imp
            * (v) CAA, 2015-  Merged PIO and OCI schemes.
            * (vi) CAA 2019
            * Provided a fast-track citizenship process (naturalization in 5 years instead of 11) for persecuted religious minorities (Hindus, Sikhs, Buddhists, Jains, Parsis, and Christians) from Pakistan, Bangladesh, and Afghanistan who entered India before Dec 31, 2014.
            * Excluded Muslims from these 3 countries from the benefit.
            * Exempted such migrants from being classified as illegal immigrants.
            * Not applicable to tribal areas of the Northeast and certain regions under ILP (Inner Line Permit- Manipur, Nagaland, Arunachal and Mizoram- NagMani ArMi ).
	Acquisition of Citizenship


In India, citizenship is granted by the Ministry of Home Affairs (Central Government) according to the rules and laws regulated by Parliament
	1. By Birth: Automatic
- Before 1 July 1987: Citizen irrespective of parents' nationality. 
- 1 July 1987 – 2 Dec 2004: Either parent must be an Indian citizen at birth time. 
- After 3 Dec 2004: Both parents must be Indian citizens or one Indian + the other not an illegal immigrant. 
- Exceptions: Enemy aliens and children of foreign diplomats in India are not granted citizenship by birth. 
2. By Descent: 
- If born outside India on/after 3 Dec 2004, not a citizen unless registered at an Indian consulate within one year. 
- Requires permission from the Central Government after one year. 
3. By Registration: (Eligible categories) 
- Indian Origin Resident: Lived in India for 7 years before applying. 
- Indian Origin Abroad: Residing outside undivided India. 
- Spouse of Indian Citizen: Married + resident in India for 7 years before applying. 
- Minor Child: Born to Indian citizens. 
- Person with Indian Parent(s): Either parent was an Indian citizen at independence; must reside in India for 12 months before applying. 
4. By Naturalization: 
- Applicants must renounce foreign citizenship and not belong to a country that prohibits Indian naturalization. 
- Must reside in India or serve in its government for 12 months before applying. 
- In the preceding 12 years, you must have lived in India for at least 11 years. 
- Post-naturalization, must reside in India or serve in an Indian-recognized intnl org. 
5. By Incorporation of Territory: 
- When a foreign territory is added to India, its residents become Indian citizens (e.g., Pondicherry, 1962).
	Termination of Citizenship
	1. By Renunciation: 
- If an Indian citizen voluntarily renounces citizenship, their minor child also loses citizenship unless one parent remains Indian. 
- A child who loses citizenship can regain it after attaining legal adulthood. 
2. By Termination: 
- If an Indian acquires foreign citizenship voluntarily, Indian citizenship is automatically terminated. 
- Exception: Does not apply during war. 
3. By Deprivation (Government action): 
- Citizenship can be revoked by the Central Government under Section 10 of the Citizenship Act, 1955. 
- Grounds include: 
  - Obtained by fraud. 
  - Disloyalty to the Constitution (act/speech against India). 
  - Imprisonment in India for 2+ years within the first 5 years of acquiring citizenship. 
  - Living abroad for 7+ years without declaring intent to retain Indian citizenship. 
- Proper safeguards exist to ensure due process.
	Overseas Citizenship of India (OCI)
	- Introduced as an alternative to dual citizenship (which India does not allow). 
- Merged with Person of Indian Origin (PIO) in 2015; now only OCI exists. 
- No need for a visa to travel to India; OCI card suffices. 
- No voting rights, government jobs, or agricultural land ownership. 
- Can open special NRI like bank accounts, make investments, adopt children in India. 
- Now requires renewal only once at 20 years of age.
	Non Resident Indian (NRI)
	- Indian passport holders residing abroad for work, business, or education. 
- Must stay outside India for 183+ days in a year to qualify as an NRI. 
- NRIs cannot buy agricultural or plantation land in India.
	Person of Indian Origin (PIO) 
	- Foreign citizens whose ancestors were Indian citizens/ permanent residents after the Constitution’s commencement. 
- PIO category was discontinued in 2015, m
erged into OCI.
	OCI vs NRI vs PIO
	- OCI: Lifelong visa-free travel to India; cannot vote, hold government jobs, or buy farm land. 
- NRI: Indian citizens abroad; can invest in India. Can vote but they just can’t vote remotely or by proxy unless specifically allowed. 
- PIO: No longer exists; merged with OCI in 2015.
	7. Fundamental Rights
            * Types of rights
            * Natural rights
            * By virtue -by Birth -Society independent -Universal
            * John Locke- Life, liberty and property
            * Ex- SK Kaul led SC bench => Right to property= natural + Constnl + legal right
            * Legal right
            * By customs, statutes- Society dependent
            * India = civil, criminal, personal laws
            * Ex - ABORTION rights, transgender rights etc
            * Constitutional rights
            * Constitutionally guaranteed
            * Ex - RT Vote 


Overview of FR
            * Fundamental means = imp all round dev (material, intellectual, moral, spiritual)
            * Written in constitution
            * No law against it by Parliament
            * Called "Magna carta" of india
            * Types
            * -ve (liberties)= limits authority of state- John Locke - "when there's no law , there is no freedom”. Cases reinforcing FRs-
            * Art 19- Shreya Singhal v. UOI (2015): Struck down Section 66A IT Act, reaffirming free speech as a supreme right.
            * Art 21- Maneka Gandhi v. UOI (1978): Expanded Article 21 to include the right to live with dignity. 
            * Art 25- Shayara Bano v. UOI (2017): Struck down Triple Talaq, reinforcing religious freedom within constitutional limits.
            * +ve (entitlement)- These rights require the state to actively promote welfare and equality. Cases interpreting FRs-
            * Art 21A- Mohini Jain v. State of Karnataka (1992): Recognized education as a Fundamental Right.
            * Art 15(4) & 15(5)- Indra Sawhney v. Union of India (1992): Upheld OBC reservations in govt jobs to ensure social justice.
            * Art 39 A- Hussainara Khatoon v. State of Bihar (1979): Led to free legal aid for underprivileged prisoners.
            * Not absolute- reasonable restriction- Thomas Paine’s words ring true: "Eternal vigilance is the price of liberty.”
	

            * State definition ( A.12 )
            * According to Max Weber, state = "community of persons occupying definite territory , independent of external control, with organized govt.
            * Executive and legislative and NOT judiciary
            * 3 govt tiers- Centre, state, Local govt
            * Statutory, non statutory authority ( LIC, ONGC etc )
            * According to SC, even a private body working if they are functionally and financially controlled by states falls under the meaning of state under art 12.
            * Autonomous societies like NCERT, niti, aiims, icar, cooperatives are not part of the state.
            * Laws inconsistent with FR ( A 13 )= all laws inconsistent with, or in derogation of any of the Fundamental Rights shall be void. It implicitly provides Doctrine of “Judicial Review” for: ( word not in Constitution)
            * Permanent and temporary laws ( ordinances )
            * Statutory instruments - delegated legislation, by-laws
            * Non-legislative sources having force of law. Ex naga customary laws
            * CAA is NOT a law under art 13. (But it can be challenged under JR  if it is ultra vires FRs)
            * Freedom
            * Absence of restraints
            * Everybody enjoys , yet others'  freedom not undermined + Law & order is maintained.
            * Ex- peaceful protest, but blocking pub roads ( shaheenn bagh case )
Right to equality (Article 14)
Equality before law ( by Dicey )
	Equal protection of law
	British concept
	American concept
	No person is above law. 
Absence of special privileges
	Equal treatment under equal circumstance
Like should be treated alike.
	Equal subjection to the law of all. 
Ex: CrPC
Coparcenary rights under the Hindu Succession Act, 1956 (amended in 2005) ensure gender equality by granting daughters the same inheritance and social status as sons in ancestral property.
	Similar application of same laws with similarly situated persons. 
States can treat unequal differently to create a level playing field.
Ex: SC/ST PoA act 1989, Civil code, immunities to president and governor.
	-ve concept . Restrict state - ( does not allow equalizing measures like reservation.)
	+ve- obligate state for reinforcing equity- Ex- reservation 
	            * Rule of Law by AV Dicey
            * Means "one act for all & one judiciary for all"
            * 3 COMPONENTS
            * Equal subjection
            * No arbitrary Power, punishment sans law breach
            * Primacy of individual rights, Constitution= result of rights of individual defined, enforced by law. Not applicable - India. Here Constitution= source of all individual rights
            * Art 14- Tests both procedural and substantial & substantive validity (unlike Rule by law where only procedural validity is checked) 
            * SC= basic feature
            * Any Legislation confering on exec / admin, an  auth unguided and uncontrolled discretionary power in application of law, violates A 14 
            * Not absolute but has Reasonable restrictions to Art 14- Constitutional immunities
            * A. 361= President and governor
            * President & governor immune to criminal proceedings in both personal & official capacity; civil proceedings only in official capacity. 
            * In personal capacity for civil acts done before & after joining the office, at least 2 month notice needed for civil proceedings. 
            * Article 361A provides protection to authors and publishers of true reports on legislative proceedings and court proceedings from legal action.
            * Article 105 and 194- Parliamentary privileges- grants MPs and MLAs resp- immunity for speeches and votes in Parliament, by providing special privileges to legislators
            * Art 31C- Laws giving effect to DPSPs under art 39 (b) and © cannot be challenged ultra vires article 14, 19. SC held- “where article 31 C comes in, article 14 goes out- 25th CAA
            * Art 31A  = added by 1st CAA and expanded bt 4th CAA save 5 types related to land, corporate and mining ( on ground - 14,19)
            * Vienna convention- Foreign sovereigns, diplomats , ambassador . UNO, its org enjoys diplomatic immunity. (Civil, criminal)


Art 15- No discrimination- basis (race , religion, caste, sex, place of birth)
            * No disability, liability, restriction wrt
            * Art 15 (1) Private- Access to shops, pub restaurant, hotels, place of public entertainment.
            * Art 15 (2) Public- Use of wells, tanks, bathing ghats maintained wholly/ partly out of state funds
            * 4 Reasonable restrictions
            * Art 15 (3) States can make special provision for women and children. Ex - reservation for women in local bodies, RT free education for children 
            * Article 15(4) -  Special provisions for SEBCs (socially & educationally backward classes) (SCs & STs) for education . EX - RESERVATION of seats & Fee concession
            * Article 15(5)- Special provisions for SEBCs (OBCs) for edu- for educational institutions including private institutions like IITs, IIMs The 93rd CAA added Article 15(5) with a built-in exception to preserve minority rights under Article 30(1). (27% quotas for OBCs)
            * Criteria for creamy layer - kids of consti posts, Group A and B civil servants, 8 lakh. 
            * Ramnandan committee identified OBC Creamy layer
            * Article 15(6)- Special provisions for EWS for edu including private institutions, provision was added by 103rd CAA (10% Quotas) 
Article 16- Equality of Opportunity in public employment 
            * Discrimination prohibited grounds ( race, rlgn, caste, sex, place of birth, Descent, residence )
            * Reasonable restrictions- 
            * Art 16 (3)- Residence as condition for certain employment. Ex - in Andhra & Telangana
            * Article 16(4) – reservation for SEBCs, SCs, and STs in govt jobs
            * Article 16(4A) – reservation in promotions for SCs and STs in govt jobs.
            * Art 16(5)-  exception to incumbent religious institutions. 
            * Article 16(6) – Provides reservation for EWS in public employment 
            * Reservations are not FRs but Consti provides for reservations for social justice
            * Reservation ≠ Fundamental Right: The SC in Mukesh Kumar ruled that denying a quota is not a violation of constitutional rights; Article 16(4) provides reservations for backward classes.
            * Promotion & Carry Forward: Article 16(4A)allows SC/ ST reservation in promotions if underrepresented, while 16(4B) permits carrying forward unfilled quotas for up to 5 years. (Note For OBCs, backlog vacancies are not subject to the creamy layer restriction, ensuring that reserved posts are filled.)
            * Key SC Judgments:
            1. Indira Sawhney: No need to prove backwardness once listed under Articles 341, 342.
            2. M. Nagaraj: States not bound to provide promotion quotas; benefits should go to the weakest.
            3. Jarnail Singh (2018): No backwardness test for SC/ST, but creamy layer applies; states need not prove backwardness-. 
            * BR ambedkar - reservation sans qualifiers could eat up RT Equality 
            * Suggestion to overhaul the reservation system w/o disrupting the present system- reservation can be availed only once, either in Education institutions or in public employment. 


Article 17- Abolition of untouchability as well as its practice in any form
            * Term = undefined - Constitution
            * ONLY ABSOLUTE RIGHT - No exceptions and avail against private individuals
            * Only parliament can legislate.
            * Protection of Civil Rights Act, 1955.
            * Punishment: 6 months imprisonment + fine.
            * Conviction leads to disqualification from elections to Parliament/ State Legislature.
            * Mysore HC = not incl boycott few indiv


Article 18- Abolition of titles except military, academic
            * States can confer titles like padma bhushan etc. SC upheld National awards validity, but not use prefix/ suffix
            * Padma Bhushan and Bharat Ratna awards were suspended twice in 1977 and 1992 
            * Citizen can't accept foreign title
            * Foreigners in pub office  can't accept foreign title without prez consent

Article 19- Right to freedom
               * 19- Freedom of ( speech hogi assembly me jha association move or reside karenge professors k saath)
               * Originally 7 rights, RT property deleted by 44th CAA (Morarji Desai Govt) 
               * Article 19 (1) (a)- Speech & expression  includes, in any mode =  words, signs, symbols, gestures, pictures 
               * Freedom is defined in such a way that everybody enjoys their freedom and that of others is not undermined and law AND order prevails. 
               *  puo "odeu

OOUOI!S                 * CPI (M) vs Bharat Kumar case = bandh (strike) not within freedom of expression 
               * Shreya singhal vs UoI case => section 66A read down ( violate 19 )
               * Anuradha bhasin v UoI - Right to internet under art 19 and 21.
                  * Propounded "doctrine of Proportionality test"
                  * Legitimate action- Is the action necessary?
                  * Least restrictive- Is there a less harmful way to achieve the goal?
                  * Rational nexus- Does the action logically connect to the goal?
                  * Gherao  = group surround official, building, prevent from leaving until demands not met-  Not protected by A 19 ( 1)(b)
                  * Reasonable restrictions- Sovereignty and integrity of India, Security of State, friendly relations with foreign states, public order, decency or Morality, contempt of court, defamation and incitement of an offence.
                  * Article 19(1)(b)- Freedom of Assembly
                  * Reasonable restrictions - 
                  * Section 141 BNS-  more than 5 ppl gathering is illegal if resist execution of law
                  * IPC 144 = magistrate can restrict gathering if obstruction to justice , annoyance, human life in danger
                  * Ramlilla maidan vs UoI 2012 - Citizen = FR peaceful protest, cant taken away
                  * The Shaheen Bagh judgment reaffirmed that FRs under Article 19 are not absolute and must be balanced with the rights of other citizens and the state’s legitimate interests (blocking public roads).
                  * Article 19(1)(c)- Freedom of Association-  Gives all citizens the right to form associations/ union/ coop societies or the right to not join any association. However, the right to obtain recog of association is not a fundamental right. 
                  * Reasonable restrictions- Sovereignty and integrity of India; Public order and Morality. 
                  * Article 19(1)(d)- Freedom of Movement- India is one union and promotes national feeling. Protects only internal movt. (External movt - RT travel abroad is protected under art 21- Maneka gandhi case)
                  * Reasonable restrictions
                  * Interest of General public- SC on movt of prostitutes on grounds of morality; Bombay high court restriction of AIDS 
                  * Interest of STs- Ex: ILP
                  * Article 19 (1) (E)- Residence- Complementary to right to movement
                  * Reasonable Restriction
                  * Interest of General public- prostitutes, habitual offenders prevented 
                  * Interest of STs- to protect distinct cultures. 
                  * Article 19 (1) (F) freedom of Profession
                  * Reasonable restriction - 
                  * Interest of General public- 
                  * Wide scope, but immoral (Trafficking) & dangerous occupations are excluded.
                  * Powers of state- 
                  * prescribe professionals, technical qualifications 
                  * Carry on itself any trade, business, service (monopolise) 
                  * Article 304(b) allows state legislatures to impose reasonable restrictions on the freedom of trade, commerce, and intercourse within the state in the public interest.  But prez consent is necessary.
                  * Originally Property was FR under article 19, but removed by 44th CAA, 
                  * Amendment removed obligation of  compensation, although Supreme court provided compensation is inferred from Article 300A


Article 20- Protection in respect of conviction of offences (only criminal cases)
                  * No ex - post facto law- No law shall declare any action from backdate as illegal. Article 20(1) does not apply to preventive detention, as it is not a punishment for a past act, but a measure for future prevention.
                  * No double jeopardy- Not punished more than once same offence (Not applicable in administrative or departmental authorities as they aren’t of judicial nature) 
                  * No self incrimination- No person shall be forced to give evidence against himself. 
                  * Doesn’t extend to compulsory production of material objects, give thumb impressions, signature & blood specimens & exhibition of body. 
                  * FYI- Audi Alteram Partem" is a principle of natural justice which means "hear the other side." before making a decision.


Article 21 - Protection of life & liberty
                  * No person shall be deprived of his life & liberty except according to procedure estb by law. It is available against arbitrary action of legislature & executive. 
                  * 1st Gen Rights→ Liberty & protection from the State (e.g., speech, vote, fair trial).
                  * 2nd Gen Rights → Welfare duties of the State (e.g, education, health, work).
                  * Widened interpretation of RT life & personal liberty
                  * AK gopalan case  (1950 )- 
                  * Took narrow interpretation of art 21 where it upheld procedure established by law. It held that protection under art 21 is available only against arbitrary action of executive and not legislature.
                  * Maneka Gandhi case ( 1978 )
                  * Took wider interpretation. Intro - American 'due process of law'  provided protection against legislative action too 
                  * It stated that procedure estd by law assumes law is always right while due process of law can question if law itself is reasonable, fair, just. 
                  * Right to live with human dignity.
                  * Political Rights:
                  * Travel Abroad: Maneka Gandhi case.
                  * Right to Information- UoI vs Asscn for Democratic Reforms 2002. 
                  * Right to be forgotten 
                  * Economic Rights:.
                  * Right to livelihood
                  * Right to Shelter: Chameli Singh v. State of UP (1996) (Housing as a basic need).
                  * Social Rights:
                  * Right to Health: Paramanand Katara case (Timely medical assistance)
                  * Right to free education upto 14 years of age-  Mohini Jain v. State of KT
                  * Right to marry freely- Shafin Jahan Case
                  * X vs NCT of Delhi (2022): Allowed termination of pregnancies (20–24 weeks) under changed material circumstances
                  * Reputation & Defamation: Subramanian Swamy vs UOI (2016): Upheld criminal defamation, balancing free speech with the right to reputation.
                  * LGBTQ Rights: Navtej Singh Johar case (Decriminalization of homosexuality).
                  * Environmental Rights:
                  * MC Mehta vs. UoI (1986) (Oleum Gas Leak Case) – Established the Absolute Liability Principle, holding industries strictly liable for environmental damage without exceptions. Ex: EPA 1986.
                  * Subhash Kumar vs. State of Bihar (1991) – Declared that the Right to a Pollution-Free Environment under Article 21
                  * Vellore Citizens Welfare Forum vs. UoI (1996) – Recognized the Precautionary Principle and Polluter Pays Principle as part of environmental law. Ex: Waste mgmt guidelines.
                  * TN Godavarman Thirumulpad vs. Union of India (1996) – Expanded the definition of forests, leading to stricter forest conservation. Eg- Compensatory Afforestation Fund Act, 2016
                  * MC Mehta vs. Kamal Nath (1997) (Public Trust Doctrine) – Held that natural resources are public property, and the government must act as a trustee to protect them for future generations.
                  * M.K. Ranjitsinh. Vs. UoI- recognised the right to 'be free from the adverse effects of climate change' under Article 21
                  * Technological Rights:
                  * Right to Internet Access: Kerala HC (Fahima Shireen) & SC (Anuradha Bhasin) (Digital rights & freedom of expression).
                  * Right to Privacy: Puttaswamy judgement (Data protection & personal autonomy).
                  * Legal & Criminal Justice Rights:
                  * Right against Public Hanging & Handcuffing: Protection of human dignity.
                  * Right against custodial torture- DK Basu case (1997)
                  * No Double Jeopardy, No Ex-Post Facto Law, No Self-Incrimination: Article 20 protections.
                  * Bail Principle: State of Rajasthan v. Balchand (1977) ("Bail is the norm, jail is the exception").
                  * Rajendra Prasad: “A Constitution like a machine is a lifeless thing. It acquires life because of the men who control it.”
                  * Article 21A- RTE  ( 86th CAA )
                  * RTE: Guarantees free and compulsory education for all children aged 6–14 years under Article 21A of the Constitution.
                  * Universal Right: Applies to every child, regardless of income, caste, religion, or gender. (NOT just BPL)
                  * Definitions: Defines a child (6–14 years) and what qualifies as a school under the Act.
                  * No Denial of Admission: Admission cannot be denied due to lack of documents or age proof.
                  * Age-Appropriate Admission: If a child has never been to school or dropped out, they must be admitted to a class appropriate for their age, with special training provided to help them catch up.
                  * 25% Reservation: Private, unaided schools must reserve one-fourth of their seatsfor children from economically weaker sections and disadvantaged groups.
                  * No Screening or Capitation Fees: Schools cannot interview children or parents for admission, nor can they charge "donation" fees.
                  * Ban on Harassment: The Act strictly prohibits physical punishment, mental harassment, and discrimination.
                  * No Detention (Revised): Originally, no child could be failed until Class 8. However, a 2019 amendment now allows states to hold regular exams in Classes 5 and 8 and potentially detain students who do not pass a re-examination.
                  * Pupil-Teacher Ratio (PTR): Mandates a ratio of 1:30 for primary and 1:35 for upper primary levels to ensure individual attention.
                  * Infrastructure: Schools must meet specific standards, including separate toilets for boys and girls, safe drinking water, a playground, and a library.
                  * Teacher Norms: Teachers must have the required professional qualifications and are prohibited from giving private tuitions.
                  * School Management Committees (SMCs): Government schools must form committees—mostly comprising parents—to monitor school functioning and manage development plans.
Procedure estb by law
	Due process of law
	AK Gopalan case 1950- Based on assumption that laws are always right. Checks arbitrariness of Executive only.
	Maneka Gandhi case 1978- Checks arbitrariness of both executive and legislature. Check if laws are just and resonable.
	Narrow scope- Judiciary assess only substantive question, ie competency to frame law & if it follows process, not instinct
	Broad scope- Substantive grounds + procedural ground of unreasonability- Instinct checked. Fairer treatment - indiv rights 
	Japan
	American Constitution
	Mentioned in constitution. 
	Not mentioned in the constitution.
	

Articles 22- Freedom against arrest and detention (only for criminal & quasi criminal cases) 
                  * Ordinary (Punitive) Detention: Article 22(1) & 22(2)
                  * The Concept: Detention takes place only after a formal trial and conviction in a court of law.
                  * Right to Grounds: The arrestee must be informed immediately of the reasons for their arrest.
                  * Right to Counsel: The arrestee has a fundamental right to consult and be defended by a legal practitioner of choice.
                  * Magistrate Production: The arrestee must be produced before the nearest magistrate within 24 hours (excluding journey time).
                  * Release Rule: The arrestee must be released after 24 hours unless the magistrate authorises further detention.
                  * Preventive Detention: Article 22(3) to 22(7)
                  * The Concept: Detention is based on suspicion to prevent a future crime, skipping a trial and conviction.
                  * Right to Counsel Denied: Article 22(3)(b) explicitly denies the right to consult or be defended by a lawyer.
                  * Right to Grounds: The detenu must be informed of the grounds of detention.
                  * Public Interest Exception: The state can refuse to disclose facts if doing so goes against public interest.
                  * Right to Representation: The detenu must be given the earliest opportunity to make a representation against the detention order.
                  * Time Limit: Detention cannot exceed 3 months unless an Advisory Board (including High Court judges) approves an extension.
                  * The 44th Amendment Fact: The 44th Constitutional Amendment Act (1978) reduced this limit to 2 months, but it has not been brought into force.
                  * Lawmaking Powers
                  * Shared Authority: Both Parliament and State Legislatures (SL) can enact preventive detention laws.
                  * Exclusive Parliamentary Authority: Parliament has exclusive power for laws regarding Defence, Foreign Affairs, and Security of India.
                  * Concurrent Authority: Both Parliament and State Legislatures can make laws regarding the Security of a State, Public Order, and Maintenance of Essential Supplies & Services.
                  * Parliamentary Overrides: Parliament alone can prescribe the circumstances/classes of cases for detention beyond 3 months, specify the maximum period, and set Advisory Board procedures.
                  * Statistical Data: The NCRB ‘Crimes in India’ Report 2021 recorded over 1.1 lakh persons under preventive detention, the highest since 2017.
                  * Safeguards not applied to enemy alien, person detained under any law of preventive detention
                  * Cases
                  * AK Gopalan case 1950- SC upheld legality of preventive detention
                  * Ambedkar's justification - " In present circumstances, necessary for the executive against tampering with pub order, defense. This is exigency of liberty not abv pub interest. "


RA exploitation (Article 23-24) 
                  * 23= Prohibition of trafficking in human beings and forced labour
                  * Absolute prohibition- Devadasi included.
                  * Immoral traffic prevention act 1956,  factories act 1948, mines act 1952, apprentice Act 1961 - by parliament to punish. 
                  * Exception - Not prevent the state to impose compulsory pub service w/o pay. But no discrimination - rlgn, race, caste, class.
                  * 24 = Prohibition of employment of children in factories.
                  * Child labour prohibition act - Prohibits employment of children below 14 years in hazardous activities, but does not prohibit in harmless or innocent work. 
                  * In 2006- GoI banned employment of children  under 14 as domestic servants, workers in dhabas, shops, factories


RT freedom of rlgn (25-28)
                  * Article 25-  freedom of conscience & profession, practice, propagation freely
                  * Cover religions belief (doctrine), practices ( rituals )
                  * Art 25 contains 2 explanations- 1. Sikhs carrying kirpans; 2. Hindus = sikh, jain, buddhist
                  * State can regulate/ restrict/ reform religious eco, fin, pol activity-. Ex Sabarimala Women temple entry, Abolition of Triple talaq. 
                  * Constitution doesn’t define minority, but recognize rlgs, linguistic minority 
                  * India is an unusual nation where the propagation of religion is both a fundamental right and a criminal offence
                  * Reasonable restrictions- Public order, morality, Health and other provisions related to FRs.
                  * Article 26 - manage religious affair 
                  * 25 - indiv rights,  while 26 - collective rights
                  * Restriction = pub order, morality, health
                  * Acc to SC Condition for religious denomination-  Hence RK mission, Ananda  Marg= religious denomination, Aurobindo society  is not. 
                  * Belief system conducive 4 spiritual well- being
                  * Common org
                  * Have distinct name
                  * Article 27- Freedom from taxation for promotion of religion
                  * State should not spend public money collected by Tax for promotion/ maintenance of particular religion, but can be spent for All region's promotion
                  * Fees can be levied. 
                  * Restrictions - Gov't can't compel anybody to pay tax for propagation, Maintenance of religion.
                  * Article 28 -Freedom from attending religious instruction in certain edu inst
                  * 4 types of educational institutions  -
                  * Wholly state maintained - religious instruction is prohibited
                  * Estd by a trust, State admin, - permit (article 28 does not apply)  
                  * State recog (DAV) + state aid (KVS) = voluntary basis


Cultural & edu rights (29, 30) 
                  * Article 29 - any section of citizens residing in any part of India having distinct lang, script, culture shall have the right to conserve the same. (Linguistic and religious minority)
                  * No citizen shall be denied admission in state maintained educ inst on grounds only of race, religion, caste & language
                  * Right to agitate for protection of lang, promise to conserve language not amount to corrupt practices under RPA 1951 
                  * Word “minority” is not defined
                  * Aligarh Muslim University Case Verdict 2024
                  * Government can regulate minority institutions under Article 19(6), but such regulation must not infringe their minority character.
                  * Minority status is retained even if the institution is governed by non-minorities or subject to state regulation.
                  * Article 30 - Right of minority to estb & admin edu institutes
                  * All minorities( religious + language)  can establish education insti of their choice (Rights only to ensure minority = majority and does not give spcl privileges to minorities.)
                  * Compensation amount fixed by state for compulsory acquisition of any property of minority educ inst (44th CAA) 
                  * No state discrimination for fund
                  * If institution seeks state recognition or aid, then the state can regulate syllabus, sanitation ,staff employment, governing body without altering the character of minority insti. 
                  * If insti does not seek recog/ aid- it is only subject to general laws  like contract laws, labour law, tax laws etc).
                  *  CHARTER OF EDU RIGHTS- Madrasas administered by this article
                  * Not absolute , Reasonable restrictions- 
                  * State can provide Regulatory regime in national interest
                  * Provide well-qualified teacher
                  * National commission for minority edu instn
                  * NCMEI act 2004 - statutory
                  * Head = chairman ( HC judge )
                  * 3 member, centre nominated
                  * Fn = complaints of violation of minority rights
                  * Status of Right to property
                  * Now only Constitutional/ legal right under Art 300A to reduce obstacles in land acquisition for public welfare. - No person deprived except by authority of law. 
                  * Can be regulated, curtailed, abridged without CAA by an ordinary law of parliament
                  * Article 300 provides protection against executive action but not against legislative action . So if violation of right, then article 226 can be revoked
                  * No guaranteed right to compensation in case of acquisition/ reacquisition of property
                  * 2 cases when compensation must be paid - 
                  * Article 30- Agricultural land.  
                  * Article 31 A- where land under personal cultivation is within statutory ceiling limits
                  * 44th Amendment omit A 31, 19(1)(f) , inserted in their place Art 300A
                  * Exceptions to FRs
                  * 31 A - saves 5 types of laws from being challenged against Article 14 & 19
                  * Relating to property, amalgamation of corporations, share holding , mining lease, estate rights. 
                  * Article 31A does not protect a State law unless it is reserved for the President’s consideration and receives his assent.
                  * 31 B  - saves acts in 9th schedule on grounds of contravention of ANY FR (1st CAA)
                  * IR Coelho case (2007)- SC ruled that there couldn’t be any blanket judicial review of such laws in 9th schedule + Judicial Review is a basic feature.  Article- 14,15,19,21 forms part of basic structure 
                  * Presently 284 laws under 9th Schedule. 
                  * 31 C - saves laws giving effect to Directive principles under Article 39 B/C on grounds of contravention of Article 14,19  (25th CAA)


                  * RT constitutional remedies ( Art 32 )
                  * Ambedkar = most important article & soul of constitution. Mere declaration of fundamental rights is useless, worthless w/o enforcement mechanism.
                  * The SC Ruled it as Basic Feature and cant be abridged. There can be No law defying FR as they’re Guaranteed against law.
                  * 4 provisions under Article 32 
                  * Guaranteed Right to  move SC for enforcement. 
                  * SC has Power to issue writs/ order 
                  * Parliament can empower any other courts to issue  direction, order, writs (DOW )
                  * Not suspend except as provided by const- i.e. - Prez  can suspend during national emergency - A. 359 
                  * Limitations
                  * If State violates FRs = SC provides remedy under art 32; Indiv violate = HC under 226,  not SC directly. Exception- Under Articles 15(2), 17, 23, and 24.. As they apply to private also, so even if individuals violate, they can go to SC.
                  * Art 32 only for FRs whereas HC = greater jurisdiction as provide writs for ordinary laws too
                  * Art 32 cant be invoked simply to determine constitutionality of executive order/ legislation until it directly 
                  * SC  and HC= original, not exclusive jurisdiction. 
                  * Romesh thappar vs madras ( 1950 )
                  * Article 32 is a “guaranteed” remedy against FR violation. Hence SC  guarantor, defender
                  * Enforcement of FR 
                  * FRs have vertical application → Enforceable against the State, not individuals. Ex - Article 19
                  * Some rights have horizontal application → Enforceable against individuals too (Art 15 (2), 17 , 23, 24).
                  * Most FR are self-executory, but some need a Parliament-made law (Articles 17, 23, and 24) for enforcement to ensure national uniformity (Article 35).


Note- 
there are five primary articles have Horizontal application meaning they are enforceable against private individuals along with state:
                  1. Article 15(2): Right to access shops, hotels, and public places without discrimination.
                  2. Article 17: Abolition of untouchability.
                  3. Article 21: Right to Life and Liberty (enforceable horizontally per the Kaushal Kishore ruling).
                  4. Article 23: Prohibition of human trafficking and forced labour.
                  5. Article 24: Prohibition of child labour in hazardous environments.


The Fundamental Rights exclusive to citizens only are Articles 15, 16, 19, 29, and 30.


Writs
                  * From british = prerogative law ,
                  * Origin = regulating act 1773
                  * Def = extraordinary power to SC, HC as guarantor
                  * Parliament can order too, but no provision yet
                  * Jurisdictions of SC & High court 
SC
	HC
	Narrow Jurisdiction- Only for FR
	Wider jurisdiction- FRs + Constitutional + statutory rights
	Wider territorial jurisdiction- Against person, govt all over India
	Narrower territorial jurisdiction- In territory only ( limited territorial jurisdiction )
	Cannot be refused- Article 32 is itself a FR, but the SC may refuse to issue a writ if an alternative, effective remedy exists.
	May refuse as A.226 - discretionary
	







Types of writs-
Writ
	Purpose
	Can Be Issued Against
	Cannot Be Issued Against
	Habeas Corpus
("to have the body")
	- To release a person unlawfully detained
- Can be filed by the detainee or others (if detainee unable)
	- State authorities: Executive, Police, Jail authorities
- Private individuals (in cases of private illegal detention)
	- If detention is lawful
- If court has jurisdiction
- Detention by a competent court
- Contempt of court or legislature
	Mandamus
("we command")
	- To compel public authority to perform constitutional/statutory/public duty
	- Executive authorities (Police, Bureaucracy)
- Public Corporations (Municipalities, Universities)
- Lower judiciary (not higher courts)
	- Private individuals/entities
- President, Governor
- CJI and High Court judges acting in judicial capacity
- MPs/MLAs/Ministers
- Discretionary/contractual duties
	Quo Warranto
("by what authority")
	- To challenge illegal/usurped occupation of a public office
- Any Public spirited individual.
	- Any person holding a substantive, public, statutory office
- Must be of public nature with public duty, not honorary/ temporary
	- Private offices
- Offices not created by Constitution/statute
- If office is not of public importance
	Prohibition
(Preventive)
	- Issued by higher court to stop lower court/quasi-judicial body from exceeding jurisdiction
	- Lower courts
- Tribunals
- Quasi-judicial bodies
	- Administrative/executive authorities (like PM, CM, ECI, UPSC acting in administrative capacity)
- Legislative bodies
	Certiorari
(Preventive + Curative)
	- To quash order of lower court/tribunal for lack/excess of jurisdiction or violation of natural justice
	- Lower courts, Tribunals, Quasi-judicial bodies
- Executive authorities if performing quasi-judicial functions (post 1991 case laws)
	- Purely administrative authorities
- Legislative bodies
- Private individuals/entities
	

                  * Armed forces (article 33) 
                  * A.33 - parliament can restrict/ abrogate rights of armed forces/ police/ intelligence agency  to ensure  proper discharge of duty 
                  * Any FR restricted  inclu Article 19 
                  * Any such parliamentary law cant be challenged on the grounds of contravention of FRs
                  * Non–combatants cover
                  * Exclude court martial
                  * Article 34-  deals with the restriction of fundamental rights during the operation of martial law in any area.  (Military rule)
                  * Derived From Britain.
                  * Undefined term-  No express provision, though implicit in A 34 
                  * 34-B = FR restrict while martial law is in place. Empowers parliament to indemnify any govt servant.
                  * This act of indemnity can’t be challenged in any court on grounds of contravention of any FR. 
                  * As per SC - Martial law does not result in the suspension of writ of Habeas Corpus. 
                  * Grounds to declare martial law = war, invasion, insurrection, rebellion, riot , violent resistance to law.
                  * Justification of martial law= repeal force by force
                  * National emergency difference
                  * ML = only FR affected; NE = broader (centre state relation, FR, etc)
                  * ML = suspend govt & ordinary law court; NE - courts & govt continue. 
                  * ML= imposed to restore law & order due to any reason, NE = only 3 grounds- War, External aggression or Armed rebellion.
                  * ML= only imposed in some areas, NE = some/ entire areas both 
                  * Article 35 empowers only Parliament to make laws mentioned in 
                  * Articles 16(3)(prescribing residence as condition) , 
                  * Art 32(3)  - empowering other courts for writs
                  * Article 33, 34.
                  * As well as prescribing punishments for 
                  * Article 17
                  * Article 23
                  * Note- Unlike Article 35, for Articles 15(2)- equal access to public spaces and 24, both Centre and States make laws, as the subjects fall under the Concurrent List.
                  * Constitutional/ Legal/ Non fundamental Rights outside part 3
                  * Article 265- no tax levy except by authority of law.
                  * Article 300A (part 12)- No deprivation of property except by law
                  * Article 301 (part 13)- free trade, commerce, intercourse throughout country
                  * Article 326- election to LS, SLA= basis of universal adult sufferage 
                  * Doctrine of severability
Criticism of Fundamental Rights
Criticism
	Details
	Excessive Limitations
	As Jaspat Roy Kapoor noted, FRs come with many restrictions.
	Lack of Socio-Economic Rights
	No rights like Right to Social Security, Right to Work (unlike USSR, China).
	Ambiguity & Legal Complexity
	Vague, indefinite, and called a "paradise of lawyers" (Sir Ivor Jennings).
	No Permanency
	Can be curtailed by the President (e.g., Right to Property).
	Political & Class Bias
	Benefits the wealthy (expensive remedies) and can be misused by ruling parties.
	No Consistent Philosophy
	Criticized by Sir Ivor Jennings for lacking a unified ideological base.
	Significance of Fundamental Rights
Significance
	Details
	Ensures Individual Freedom
	Golaknath case (1967): SC held that "FR enables man to chalk out life in the manner he likes best."
	Bedrock of Democracy
	Essential for democratic governance and rule of law.
	Protection & Safeguards
	Acts as a formidable bulwark of liberty and secures material, moral well-being.
	Protects Minority & Weak
	Strengthens secularism, shields vulnerable groups from oppression.
	Checks Absolute Power
	Prevents authoritarianism and ensures balance of power.
	

                  * Conclusion
                  * Reflects ideal citizens who adhere to society. History = significant role in drafting. 
8. Directive principles of state policy
                  * Part 4, Art 36- 51- Ideals for state for policy making- Irish Constitution ( Spanish consti )
                  * Art 36- State definition
                  * Art 37- Says these principles are fundamental to governance and it shall be duty of state to apply these principles in making laws- Used to Determine constitutional validity
                  * Welfare state- Constitute Economic, social and political program. Realise high ideals- justice, liberty, equality, fraternity.
                  * Non justiciable- not legally enforceable by the courts.
                  * Ambedkar - Novel features of constitution
                  * Granville austin- DPSPs are the conscience of the constitution.
                  * Instrument of instructions (GoI act 1935)

                  1. Criticisms
                  1. No legal force
                  1. Pious superfluity (post dated cheque)
                  2. TT krishnamachari = veritable dustbin of sentiments.
                  2. Illogically arranged
                  1. Mix imp issues + socio-eco decisions
                  3. Conservative / obsolete philosophical foundation
                  1. Ivor Jennings= based on pol philosophy of 19 Cen England, deemed suitable in 12th Century. 
                  4. Cause Constitutional conflict b/w Centre & states
                  5. Reactionary nature
                  1. Party use - selfish ends (Revri culture in garb of DPSP).
                  6. Mere promises- Only impression about just power exercise to secure support of the people. 
                  2. Utility
                  1. Proper implementation = heaven ( demo + welfare state )
                  2. Beacon of light to the  courts, executive & legislature
                  3. Ensures Stability & policy continuity in domestic & foreign policies. 
                  4. Supplement FR ( pol rights )  &  DPSP ( socio-eco rights )
                  5. Enables opposition to influence + control govt operations. Hence the performance test of the government. 

                     3. Conflicts bw FR & DP- Evolution
Case
	Judgment
	Impact
	Champakam Dorairajan (1951)
	DPSPs are subsidiary to FRs but FRs can be amended.
	Led to 1st  (1951), 4th (1955), & 17th (1964) Amendments to implement some DPSPs.
(art 31A and 9th schedule)
	Golak Nath (1967)
	FRs are sacrosanct.
Parliament cannot amend FRs to imple DPSPs. 
	Parliament reacted with: 
24th CAA (1971) → Gave power to amend FRs. 25th CAA (1971) inserted art 31 © → Laws implementing Article 39(b) & (c) DPSPs cannot be challenged for violating FRs (Art 14, 19). Courts cannot question such laws.
	Kesavananda bharati case 1973
	Declaring 2nd provision of art 31 C unconstitutional on grounds Judicial review is a basic feature of the constitution.
	Parliament reacted with 42nd CAA accorded primacy to DPSPs.
	Minerva Mills (1980)
	Struck down restrictions on FRs. Indian consti is founded on the bedrock of balance between FR + DPSP = Constitution’s conscience.
	Declared that FRs are not absolute but also not fully subordinate to DPSPs.
	IR Coelho (2007)
	Balanced FR & DPSPs. Judicial review of 9th Schedule allowed.
	Nothing in the 9th Schedule can abrogate FRs. Currently, FRs hold primacy over DPSPs but parlia can amend FRs to give effect to DPSPs w/o damaging basic structure doctrine.

	Non- justiciable
