                     * Implementation of DPSP
                     1. Modernize agri - Land reform laws
                     2. Labour welfare -
                     1. Min wages/ payment of wages act 1948 
                     1. Govt (Central/State) empowered to fix/revise minimum wages for scheduled employments.
                     2. Floor wage- centre
                     3. No uniform national wage; states set rates based on local conditions.
                     4. Wages can vary by skill level (skilled > unskilled).
                     5. Wages can be set on hourly/daily/monthly basis.
                     6. Revision mandatory every 5 years to keep pace with inflation.
                     7. Allows wages in kind, where applicable.
                     8. Employers must maintain wage records/registers.
                     2. Trade union act, factory act
                     3. Maternity benefit act 1961, equal remuneration act
                     4. KHADI & village indus board
                     5. WPA 1972, Forest conservation act 1982 - A. 48A
                     6. 73, 74 CAA
                     7. Seat reservation for SC/ ST
                     * Directives outside part 4
                     1. Article 335- claims of SC/ST in Services → Claims considered while maintaining administrative efficiency.
                     2. Mother Tongue Instruction (Art 350A) → States & local bodies to ensure facilities at the primary education level.
                     3. Hindi Development (Art 351) → Union to promote Hindi as a medium for expressing India's composite culture.
9. Fundamental Duties
Aspect
	Details
	Incorporation
	Added by the 42nd CAA, 1976 on Swaran Singh Committee's recommendation.
	Placement
	Part IV-A, Article 51A (Initially 10 duties, one more added in 86th Amendment, 2002). 
	Inspiration
	Inspired by the USSR; Japan is the only country with a constitutional list of duties.
	Fundamental Duties
	1. Respect the Constitution, national flag, anthem. 
2. Follow noble ideals of the freedom struggle. 
3. Uphold & protect sovereignty, unity, integrity (preamble). 
4. Defend the country, serve when called upon. 
5. Promote harmony, fraternity (preamble), renounce practices derogatory to women. 
6. Preserve composite cultural heritage. 
7. Protect the environment, forests, wildlife, show compassion to living creatures (DPSP).
8. Develop scientific temper, humanism, and reform spirit. 
9. Safeguard public property, avoid violence. 
10. Strive for excellence in all spheres. 
11. Provide educational opportunities (Added by 86th Amendment, 2002).
	Nature of Duties
	- Moral (e.g., cherishing noble ideals). 
- Civic (e.g., respecting the Constitution).
	Legal Status
	- Not legally enforceable (non-justiciable). 
- No penalties for violation unless provided by law.
	Criticism
	- Excludes Justice verma committee recommendation (e.g., voting, paying taxes, help) -some recommendations of swaran singh committee (total 8 recommendations), but some not included - ex - penalty for not following duties, paying taxes)  
- Some are vague (e.g., "noble ideals").
- No legal enforcement, making them a mere code of conduct. 
	Significance
	-   Rights & Duties are correlative- Reminds citizens of their responsibilities. 
- Discourages anti-national & anti-social activities. 
- Supreme Court (1992): A law upholding FD is considered reasonable. 
- Parliament ONLY can impose penalties for violations.
	Verma Committee Observations
	-identify Existing laws to help implement duties: 
1. Prevention of Insults to National Honour Act (1971) – Protects national symbols.
                     * First Offence: Punishable by up to three years imprisonment, fines, or both.
                     * Subsequent Offences: Involves mandatory minimum imprisonment of one year.
2. Criminal Laws – Punish acts inciting enmity. 
3. Protection of Civil Rights Act (1955) – Penalizes caste-based offences. 
4. IPC Sections – Penalize threats to national integrity. 
5. Unlawful Activities Prevention Act (1967) – Bans unlawful organizations. 
6. Wildlife Protection Act (1972) – Prohibits illegal trade of endangered species. 
7. Forest Conservation Act (1980) – Prevents deforestation.
	FR & FD Relationship
	- FD not enforceable via Article 32 or 226. 
- State of Guj v. Mirzapur (2005) – FD & DPSP crucial for constitutional interpretation. 
- Ramlila Maidan Case (2012) – Courts should balance rights & duties.
	

AMENDMENT, BASIC STRUCTURE DOCTRINE, JUDICIAL REVIEW 
INTRO - 
                     1. Constitutional amendment - A. 368, part 20 deals = parliament's power to amend constitution & procedures.
Amendment procedure.
                     * Introduced in either House of Parliament (not in State Legislature) by a Minister or private member.
                     * No President’s permission required.
                     * The amendment process is moderate—neither as easy as in Britain nor as rigid as in the USA.
                     * Requires a special majority:
                     * More than 50% of total membership.
                     * Two-thirds of members present & voting in each House.
                     * No joint sitting in case of disagreement.
                     * If amending federal provisions, ratification by at least half of state legislatures with a simple majority is required.
                     * After passage, the bill is sent to the President for assent.
                     * 24th CAA: President must give assent; cannot withhold or return for reconsideration.
                     * After assent, the bill becomes an Act, and the Constitution stands amended 
 Types of amendment processes
                     1. Simple majority- majority of members present & voting.  (Not under art 368)
                     2. Special majority under art 368
                     3. Special majority with consent of states under art 368- Include those provisions relating to federal structure. 
Types of majority
Type
	Meaning
	Example
	Simple Majority
	50% present and voting
	Art 2- Admission for establishment of a new state.
Art 3- Formation and alteration of area, boundary or name of states.
Art 269- Abolition , creation of legislative councils.
Second schedule- emoluments allowances privileges of President, Governor, speaker, judge.
Salary, allowances of MP
Quorum in parliament
Rule of procedure in Parliament.
Fifth schedule- administration of schedule area and ST
Sixth schedule- administration of tribal areas.
Use of official language.
Citizenship- acquisition and termination.
Conferring more jurisdiction on SC
Election to parliament & state legislature. 


Note- Article 252 allows Parliament to legislate on a State List subject if two/ more states pass a resolution requesting it.


Keeping of Govt- Passing a confidence motion 
 Removal of Govt- no-confidence motion in LS.
	Effective Majority
	More than 50% of the effective strength of the House (excluding vacant seats).
	1. Removal of the Vice President under Article 67(b) requires an effective majority in Rajya Sabha.
2. Removal of Speaker/Deputy Speaker of Lok Sabha or Rajya Sabha.
	Absolute Majority
	More than 50% of the total strength of the House.
(including vacant seats).
	1. Formation of Government: A party must secure an absolute majority (273+ in Lok Sabha).
	Special Majority (Article 249)
	2/3rd of members present and voting in Rajya Sabha.
	1. Empowering Parliament to legislate on a State List subject for a limited period if the Rajya Sabha passes a resolution (Article 249).
	Special Majority
	more than 50% of the total strength of the House.
+ 2/3rd of members present and voting
	1. CAA under Article 368- FRs and DPSP
2. 3rd Schedule ; 8th Sche
Not under art 368
2. Approval of National Emergency under Article 352.
3. Approval of State Emergency (President’s Rule) beyond one year under Article 356.
4. Removal of SC or HC Judges under Article 124(4) & 217.
5. Removal of CAG under Article 148(1).
	Special majority with consent of states under art 368
	For federal provisions
	1. President- election and its manner
2. Union, states- extent of executive powers
3. Union, state- distribution of legislative powers
4. State representation in parliament (RS)
5. SC, HC
6. 7th schedule
7. GST
8. Amending Article 368 itself
	Special Majority (Art 61)
	2/3rd of the total strength of the House.
	Impeachment of the President under Article 61.
	

Criticism of amendment
                     1. No special body unlike Constitutional convention in US
                     2. State role-  
                     1. can't initiate bills/ proposals to amendment except when passing resolution requesting creation, abolition of a legislative council (A.269)
                     2. Silence on- whether states can withdraw approval after according consent
                     3. US - 3/4th state consent. India = only ½ state consent 
                     4. No joint sitting for CAA, but for ordinary bill
                     5. Sketchy provisions, so judicial interference scope.
Basic structure doctrine
Case 
	Issue
	Supreme Court Verdict
	Impact
	Shankari Prasad Case (1951)
	Challenged 1st CAA (Right to Property curtailed)= Added A.31B (9th Schedule), making laws immune from judicial review.
	SC held that A.368 includes FR, allowing Parliament to curtail or take away FR via CAA
	Established that Parliament had the power to amend Fundamental Rights.
	Sajjan Singh Case (1965)
	Challenged 7th CAA, which expanded A.31B, asserting Parliament's power to amend laws affecting FR (except A.14, A.19, A.21).
	SC reaffirmed that Parliament has authority under A.368 to amend any part of the Constitution, including FR.
	Strengthened Parliament’s amending power over FR.
	Golak Nath Case (1967)
	Challenged 17th CAA, which restricted land acquisition unless market value was paid as compensation.
	SC ruled that FR are transcendental and immutable, meaning Parliament cannot abridge any FR.
	Limited Parliament’s power to amend FR, leading to the 24th CAA (1971), which restored its amending power.
	Kesavananda Bharati Case (1973)
	Challenged 24th CAA, which allowed Parliament to abridge any FR.
	SC (7:6 majority) ruled that while Parliament can amend FR, it cannot alter the "Basic Structure" of Constitution.
	Introduced the Basic Structure Doctrine, limiting Parliament's amending power.
	Indira Gandhi v. Raj Narain (1975)
	39th CAA placed election disputes of PM, LS Speaker beyond judicial review.
	SC struck it down as a violation of democracy and rule of law.
	Basic Structure Doctrine reaffirmed.
	42nd CAA (1976)
	Tried to eliminate judicial review by declaring no amendment can be questioned in any court.
	Strengthened Parliamentary sovereignty but later struck down.
	Attempted to remove all limitations on Parliament’s power.
	Minerva Mills Case (1980)
	Challenged 42nd CAA provisions that granted unlimited amending power to Parliament And excluded judicial review.
	SC struck down A.368(4) & (5), reaffirming the Basic Structure Doctrine. Intro Doctrine of Harmonious Construction and Doctrine of Severability.
	Judicial review & FR- DPSP balance protected.
	Waman Rao vs. UoI (1981)
	

	Doctrine applies only after Kesavananda, not retrospectively.
	

	S.R. Bommai vs. UoI (1994)
	Examined arbitrary use of A.356 (President’s Rule in states).
	SC held that misuse of A.356 violates Democracy & Federalism, both part of Basic Structure.
	Strengthened federalism and limited arbitrary dismissal of state govts.
	I.R. Coelho v. State of Tamil Nadu (2007)
	9th Schedule Case – Could SC review laws placed under 9th Schedule to protect them from judicial scrutiny?
	SC ruled that laws violating the "Golden Triangle" (A.14, A.19, A.21) are still subject to review.
	Laws after Kesavananda cannot get blanket protection. Intro "Direct Impact & Effect Test".
	 
 
Constituents of basic structure of constitution 
                     * Foundational Principles
                     * Supremacy of the Constitution
                     * Sovereign, Democratic, and Republic nature
                     * Secularism
                     * Federalism
                     * Welfare State
                     * Governance Framework
                     * Parliamentary System of Government
                     * Separation of Powers between Legislature, executive & judiciary 
                     * Rule of Law
                     * Free and Fair Elections
                     * Rights and Liberties
                     * Fundamental Rights, DPSP in harmony. 
                     * Freedom & Dignity of the Individual
                     * Principle of Reasonableness
                     * Effective Access to Justice
                     * Judiciary and Constitutional Safeguards
                     * Independence of Judiciary
                     * Judicial Review
                     * Powers of SC under Articles 32 (writs), 136 (special leave), 141 (Law by SC is binding), and 142 (SC can do complete justice) 
                     * Limited Power of Parliament to Amend the Constitution (Article 368 limits)
                     * Powers of President under Article 356 (subject to judicial review)
                     * National Unity and Integrity- Unity and Integrity of India
Aspect
	Key Point
	Example
	Signi of BSD
	Prevents misuse of majority power
	Ex: Keshavnanda- Challenged 24th CAA, which allowed Parliament to abridge any FR.
	

	Ensures separation of powers
	Ex: Bulldozer justice guidelines against executive overreach
	

	Protects democracy 
	Ex: SR Bommai Case- SC held that misuse of A.356 violates Democracy & Federalism, bo
	

	Constitutionalism
	Ex: Minerva Mills Case (1980) – Struck down parts of the 42nd Amendment that curtailed judicial review.
	Criticism
	No textual basis 
	Ex: leading to varied interpretations. Series of 4 judges case
	

	Judicial overreach
	SC striking down NJAC perceived as encroachment on legislature.
	

	Restricts Parliamentary Sovereignty
	

	Way Forward
	Define BSD clearly
	Reducing ambiguity for consistent legal application.
	

	Ensure consistent application
	Avoid selective interpretation of BSD in cases.
	

	Balance judicial review with legislative authority
	Prevent judiciary from encroaching upon Parliament’s role.
	



 
Separation of power


Category
	Key Points
	Origins
	- Montesquieu: Popularized strict separation of powers.
	Need
	- Ensures liberty, prevents tyranny, and avoids power concentration.
- Safeguards public trust in governance.
	Separation of Power: 
US vs. India
	- US: Complete separation, independent executive term.
- India: Partial separation
- Article 50 (DPSP) mandates separation, yet conflicts persist. (Executive & Judiciary)
- PM & CoM perform both legislative and executive functions.
- Emergency weakens separation.
	Key Constitutional Provisions
	 - Article 13: Judicial review. Ex: Check on executive overreach
- Articles 32 & 226: Right to constitutional remedies.
- Article 50: Direct separation of Judiciary from Executive.
- Article 361: Protects President & Governors from judicial proceedings.
- Articles 105 & 194: Define Parliamentary privileges.
- Articles 122 & 212: Bar courts from inquiring into legislative proceedings.
- Article 142: Supreme Court’s power to ensure complete justice.
	Judicial Interpretation
	- Raj Narain Singh Case (1975): SC upheld delegation of power to the Executive, affirming limited SoP in India. 
- SC (2019): Lack of judicial dominance in tribunal selection committees violates SoP.
- Judiciary’s role is to interpret rather than formulate economic policy.
	Executive Overreach
	Judicial Sphere
- Bulldozer Justice: Bypassing due process erodes public trust.
- Frontier Justice (Extrajudicial killings): Violates natural justice and rule of law.
Legislative Sphere- Securities Laws (Amendment) Ordinance, 2014, was re-promulgated thrice, sparking concerns over bypassing legislative approval.
	Judicial overreach
	Executive sphere- 2G Spectrum Case (2012): The Supreme Court canceled 122 telecom licenses issued by the government, interfering in policy matters (Centre for Public Interest Litigation v. Union of India
Legislative Sphere- Stay on Farm Laws (2021): The Supreme Court stayed the implementation of three farm laws passed by Parliament, stepping into the legislative domain
	Legislative Overreach
	Judicial Sphere – 42nd Amendment (1976): The Indira Gandhi government curtailed judicial review and made constitutional amendments beyond judicial scrutiny, weakening the Supreme Court’s authority.
Executive Sphere - NCBC Act, 2018: Parliament granted itself the power to determine OBC reservations in states, infringing upon the executive’s authority to handle state-specific socio-economic classifications.
	Significance
	Same as basic structure
	Criticism of SoP in India
	- No textual basis → leads to multiple interpretations.
- Judicial activism creates conflicts between LEJ.
- Frequent ordinances bypass legislative scrutiny. DK wadhwa case 
- Difficult amendments despite societal needs.
- Conflicts with parliamentary sovereignty.
	Way Forward
	- Clarify the definition of SoP to reduce ambiguities.
- Balance power separation with governance efficiency.
- Strengthen judicial independence in tribunal selection.
- Prevent executive overreach, ensuring adherence to due process.
	

 
Aspect
	Judicial Review
	Judicial Activism
	Judicial Restraint
	Definition
	Power of the judiciary to examine the constitutionality of legislative enactments and executive orders.
	Judiciary takes a proactive role in protecting rights and promoting justice by interpreting laws innovatively.
	Judiciary limits its intervention, leaving law-making to the legislature and avoiding interference.
	Consti Basis
	SC: Art 13, 32, 136 (Special leave)
HC: Article 13, 226 (writs), 227( power of HC over subordinate courts)
	Not explicitly mentioned, derived from judicial interpretations.
	Based on the doctrine of Separation of Powers.
	Types)
	-by Justice Syed Md Quadri
- For CAA
- Legislation (Primary & Subordinate) 
- Executive Orders (Union, State, Authorities)
	- PILs ( Relaxed locus standi )
- Expanding rights (Art 21 & Environmental cases) 
- Intervening in governance failures
	- Judiciary should not interfere in policy matters 
- Respects decisions of executive & legislature
	Scope
	- Art 13- Ultra Vires FRs
- Art 21- Beyond legislative competence (Procedure estd by law)
- Violates Basic Structure Doctrine 
	-Subhash Kashyap: Legislature’s failure justifies judicial intervention (mob violence, electoral reforms) 
- Holds executive accountable
- Protects Constitutional Rights
	- Parliamentary supremacy
- Judges may lack technical expertise (Ex: Pollution cases) 


	Notable Cases
	Golaknath case (1967) – 1st application of Judicial Review
	- Hussainara Khatoon v. Bihar (1979) – Right to speedy trial for undertrials 
- Sunil Batra v. Delhi Admin (1980) – Prisoner rights & Torture case


	- Sterling Mining Case (Goa) – Judicial interference in policy-making 
- Fear of politicization (Judges’ post-retirement jobs)
	Criticism
	- Can lead to judicial supremacy(overreach of judiciary).
	- Ideological fear – Judges imposing personal values
- Epistemic fear – Do judges have technical knowledge on complex issues? (e.g., Pollution cases) 
- Democratic fear – Does PIL strengthen or weaken democracy?
	- May allow unconstitutional laws to persist 
- Courts failing to act may undermine justice
	Conclusion
	- Judicial Review ensures laws stay within constitutional limits.
	- A necessary tool for justice but should not replace legislature.
	- Essential to maintain Separation of Powers and democratic principles.
	

 
Judiciary 
                     * It's a branch of the government tasked with authoritative adjudication of controversies over application of law. 
Role of the Judiciary:
                     * Upholds the Rule of Law and settles disputes.
                     * Prevents dictatorship and ensures power separation.
                     * Protects democratic integrity (John Hart Ely).
SC- Judicial Independence ( Sop - Article 50)
                     * Appointments: President appointed in consultation of judiciary members. The Collegium system limits executive control.
                     * Security of Tenure: Removal only by impeachment.( word not used for judiciary) only on grounds mentioned in constitution.
                     * Fixed Service condition- Salaries and allowances of SC are determined by Parliament.  Not changeable to their disadvantage after appointment, except in case of financial emergency. 
                     * No fixed Tenure
                     * 65 years (62 in case of HC)
                     * Can resign by writing to president
                     * Removal by President on recommendation of parliament.
                     * Contempt Power: Can punish for disobedience.
                     * Financial Independence: Funds from Consolidated Fund of India.
                     * No Interference: Parliament can't discuss judges’ conduct (except removal).
                     * Administrative Autonomy: Manages staff, free from executive control.
                     * Post-Retirement Restriction: Judges can't plead or practice in any court within territory of India. 
                     * “Mercy to the guilty is cruelty to the victim” - Adam Smith


SC ( Article 124-147; Part 5) 
                     * Inaugurated: January 28, 1950.
                     * Estb by Indian Constitution (Commencement: January 26, 1950)
                     * Successor to Federal Court of India under Government of India Act, 1935.
                     * Jurisdiction surpasses its predecessor; replaces British Privy Council as highest court of appeal
                     * Organization
                     * The Supreme Court started in 1950 with 8 judges (1 CJI + 7 judges).
                     * Parliament decides the SC's strength through legislation (Article 124).
                     * The latest 2019 amendment increased the strength to 34 judges (1 CJI + 33 judges).
                     * Appointment
                     * CJI Appointment: 
                     * Article 124 - prez appointed in consultation  with SC, HC judges as he may deem necessary with warrant under his hand and seal, though not under his pleasure.
                     * Appointed by the President, following the seniority convention among SC judges.
                     * Other SC Judges: Appointed by the President, based on CJI’s recommendation and collegium system (CJI + 4 senior-most SC judges).
                     * First Judges Case (1982) – SC held consultation means exchange of views in judge appointments and not concurrence. (Liberalized locus standi (S.P. Gupta v. Union of India, 1981))
                     * Second Judges Case (1993) – Estd collegium system; consultation = concurrence. CJI’s advice binding on the President, given after consulting two senior judges. Seniority principle was confirmed in the 2nd judges case.
                     * Third Judges Case (1998) – SC’s opinion, not a case. Required plurality in consultation; CJI must consult 4 senior judges, If two judges dissent, the recommendation is dropped, not sent forward.
                     * Fourth Judges Case (2015) – SC struck down the 99th Amendment & NJAC Act (2014), restoring the collegium system.
                     * Famous CJIs:
                     * First: Harilal J. Kania.
                     * Longest- serving: Y.V. Chandrachud (7 years, 4 months).
                     * The 15th CAA, 1963 introduced:
                     * Article 124(2A): Parliament decides how a Supreme Court judge’s age is determined.
                     * Article 217(3): The President, after consulting the CJI, decides a High Court judge’s age, with the decision being final.
                     * Qualification of judges 124 (3)
                     * Indian citizen.
                     * High court judge for 5 years.
                     * HC advocate for 10 years (appointed directly from the bar, 9 appointed so far.)
                     * Distinguished jurist in presidents opinion ( not for HC)- Article 124
                     * no minimum age specified by constitution. 
                     * Oath
Supreme Court Judge (Article 124(6)
	President of India (article 60)
	Admin by President of India
	Chief Justice of India
	Uphold the Constitution- Perform judicial duties without fear or favor
	Preserve, protect, and defend the Constitution and law (PPD)
	Bear True faith + allegiance
	Explicit mention of faithful execution of office
	Uphold sovereignty, unity, integrity of India
	Devote himself to service and wellbeing of Indian people
	

                     * Removal process as per parliament act
Step
	Process
	Grounds (art 124(4))
	Proven misbehavior or incapacity (Only these)- Both undefined 
(but Constitution has defined term “misbehaviour” for UPSC)
Procedure of removal provided by Judge enquiry act 1968
	Initiation
	100 LS / 50 RS members must sign removal motion (almost 20%)
	Speaker/Chairman’s Role
	Can admit or reject the motion
	Inquiry Committee
	3 members (SC judge, HC CJ, distinguished jurist)
	Committee Report
	If guilty, motion proceeds to Parliament
	Parliament Approval
	Passed in both Houses separately with special majority: 
- Absolute majority of total membership 
- Two-thirds majority of members present & voting
	Presidential assent
	The President cannot reject or modify the decision.


No judge has been impeached so far. Justice V Ramaswamy was 1st case of impeachment  but motion was defeated in lok sabha
	

Provision
	Details
	Staff Appointments (Articles 146 & 229)
	- Authority: Chief Justices (CJs) of SC and HCs appoint their respective officers and servants.
- Service Conditions: Determined by the respective CJ.
- Salary Rules: Require approval from the President (for SC) or Governor (for HC).
- Administrative Expenses: Charged upon the Consolidated Fund of India (for SC) or the respective state (for HC).
	Ad Hoc Judges (Article 127)


(CQuAd-Par)
	- when there's a lack of a quorum of permanent judges.
- Appointment: CJI appoints a judge of HC temporarily qualified to be an SC judge after
                     * consulting the concerned HC CJ
                     * obtaining the President's consent.  
- Duties: Must prioritize SC sittings over other duties.
- Privileges: Possess all jurisdiction, powers, and privileges of a SC judge.
Ad Hoc Judges also receive salaries as per Parliamentary provisions.
	Retired Judges (Article 128)
	- Appointment: Retired judges of SC and HC can temporarily act as SC judges upon the CJI's request + the President's approval.
- Privileges: Enjoy jurisdiction, powers, and privileges of an SC judge but aren't considered permanent judges.  Remuneration: Entitled to salary and allowances as determined by the President.
	Acting Chief Justice of India
	- Circumstances: Appointed when the office of the CJI is vacant or the CJI is unable to perform duties due to absence, illness, or other reasons.
- Appointment: The President appoints an acting CJI.
	

                     * Jurisdiction & powers of SC
                     * SC is Final interpreter & guardian/ custodian of constitution + guarantor of FR, NOT Prez. 
Jurisdiction Type
	Scope
	Consti Provisions
	Judicial Review
	

	Article 13 
	Writ Jurisdiction
	SC has Original but not exclusive jurisdiction. It's concurrent with HC. 
Parliament can issue SC power to issue writs for other purposes only. 
	Article 32
	Court of Record
	                     * SC ( A 129) & HC ( Article 215 )are Court of record
                     * 2 provisions
                     * All judgements, proceedings, acts  ( JAP ) are  recorded. Records are evidentiary, unquestionable.
                     * The Supreme Court and High Courts, as Courts of Record, have the inherent power to investigate and punish for contempt of themselves with imprisonment up to 6 months, a fine of ₹2,000, or both. District Courts do not possess this inherent power; they must refer matters of contempt to the respective High Court for investigation and punishment.
                     * Contempt of court act 1971-
                     * The Constitution is the source of power,  and the act only defines procedure.
                     * 2 types of contempt
                     * Civil = wilful disobedience of judgements/ writs / other process of court. 
                     * Criminal =
                     * Publication of any matter / act scandalising & lowering authority of court
                     * Interfere due course - proceeding and  obstructs admin of justice
                     * Every contempt case = heard by atleast 2 judges
                     * The Supreme Court must start contempt cases within one year of the offense.
                     * Contempt of court amendment act 2006 - incl truth as defense against contempt (fair & reasonable criticism is allowed)
	Article 129
	Seat of SC 
	Delhi
CJI after Prez consent can specify to other places
	Article 130
	Original Jurisdiction
	The authority to hear cases at the first instance, not by way of appeal. This includes:
- Disputes between the GoI and one or more states.
- Disputes between two or more states.
- Cases requiring the interpretation of the Constitution or involving the enforcement of fundamental rights.
Not incl
                     * Matters referred to Finance commission
                     * Interstate water disputes
                     * Adjudge Pension & expenses between centre and state
                     * Damage recovery by state against centre
                     * Commercial nature dispute
                     * Pre constitutional treaties
                     * A dispute arising out of any consti treaty agreement which specifically provides that the said jurisdiction does not extend to such a state.
	Article 131
	Appellate Jurisdiction
	The power to hear appeals against judgments from lower courts, encompassing:
- Art 132- Constitutional matters: Appeals involving substantial questions of law (Not fact). High court has to certify that it reqs the “interpretation” of the Constitution. (High court has original jurisdiction)
- 133- Civil matters: Appeals in civil cases where a High Court certifies that the case involves a substantial question of law/ needs to be decided by SC. (No limit)
- 134- Criminal matters: Appeals in criminal cases where a HC has reversed an acquittal, sentenced an accused to death or imprisonment of 10 years.
or certified the case as fit for appeal to the Supreme Court.
- Art 136- Appeals by special leave - For any judgement (whether final or interlocutory) / any matter passed by any court, tribunal, except military court & court martial. It's a discretionary power, not claimed as a matter of right. 
	Articles 132-136
	Power to review its own judgment
	it states that the SC may review any judgment or order made by it, subject to the provisions of any law made by Parliament or rules made under Article 145 SC is a self correcting agency. For Example Kesavananda bharati, it departed from its judgement under the Golak nath case. 
{Shivdeo Singh v. State of Punjab (1963) – The SC (not constitution) held that the High Court can review its own decision under Article 226 if justice demands it.)}
	Article 137 
	Constitutional Interpretation powers
	Guided by Doctrines- 
                     * Severability – If part of a law is invalid, the rest can still stand. SC struck down part of 97th CAA relating to cooperatives.
                     * Repugnancy-  state law that conflicts with a central law on a Concurrent List subject is void to the extent of the inconsistency
                     * Pith and Substance – The core intent of a law matters more than its incidental effects. Ex - Due process of law. 
                     * Colourable Legislation – A law cannot do indirectly what it cannot do directly. (Based on SoP, applied when legislature does not have power to make law on a particular subject, but still makes one)
                     * Waiver – A person can give up their legal right voluntarily. Ex: Aruna Shanbaug v. UoI (2011) did not allow active Euthanasia as Art 21 cant be taken away under any condition.
                     * Eclipse –any rule ultra vires FR remains dormant as it is overshadowed by FRs but can be revived.
                     * Territorial Nexus – A law can apply outside a state if there’s a strong connection.
                     * Implied Powers– Powers necessary for implementing express powers are assumed. 
                     * Incidental & Ancillary Powers – Extra powers needed to enforce a law are allowed.
                     * Precedent – Courts must follow past decisions in similar cases. Kesavananda Bharati v. State of Kerala (1973) – Established the Basic Structure doctrine, shaping constitutional law.
                     * Occupied Field – If a subject is already legislated, others can’t make laws on it.
                     * Prospective Overruling– A new ruling applies to future cases, not past ones. Golaknath v. State of Punjab (1967) – Ruled that Parliament could not amend Fundamental Rights, applied only prospectively.
                     * Harmonious Construction – Conflicting laws should be interpreted to work together. Minerva Mills v. UoI (1980) – Harmonized the DPSPs with FRs, ensuring neither overrides the other; S.R. Bommai v. UoI (1994) – Balanced the President’s power to dismiss state govts (Article 356) with federalism and democracy; Shayara Bano v. UoI (2017) – Balanced personal laws (religious freedoms) with constitutional rights by striking down instant triple talaq.
                     * Liberal Interpretation – Laws should be read broadly to protect rights and justice. Maneka Gandhi v. UoI (1978) – Expanded Article 21 to include the right to a fair procedure.
                     * Doctrine of Proportionality- Nature & extent of state’s interference in a right must be proportionate to goal.
Ex1: Anuradha Bhasin v. UoI (2020)- The SC ruled that blanket shutdowns were disproportionate and violated freedom of speech. It directed periodic review of restrictions
Ex 2: K.S. Puttaswamy v. UoI- SC in Aadhar case upheld Aadhaar but struck down its mandatory use in private services, balancing state interest and individual rights.
	Article 141 – The law declared by the SC is binding on all courts in India.
Article 142 – The SC can pass any order necessary to ensure complete justice.
	Power to do complete justice 
	Article 142 empowers the Supreme Court to pass any order necessary to ensure "complete justice" in any case before it. This power is extraordinary and overrides statutory limitations, ensuring justice beyond technicalities.
Supreme Court’s Powers Under Article 142:
                     1. Enforce its orders across India (binding on all authorities- judicial superintendence).
                     2. Fill legal gaps where laws are silent or inadequate.
                     3. Issue extraordinary directions beyond statutory provisions if required for justice.
Key Case Example:
Union Carbide Case (1991) – The SC used Article 142 to award $470 mn compensation to Bhopal gas tragedy victims, overriding procedural limitations.
Rupa Ashok Hurra case 2002- SC estd Principles governing Curative Petition
Recent Examples: Chandigarh mayor elections nullified (2024), bulldozer justice guidelines issued.
	Article 142
	Consultative/ Advisory Jurisdiction
	The President of India can seek the SC's opinion on
                     * ques of law/ fact or matters of public imp which has arisen or likely to arise 
                     * Dispute arising out of Pre constitution treaty 
The advice so tendered is not binding on the president. Also, SC has no obligation to give advice except in matters of pre constitution treaty. 
So far 15 such references. 
	Article 143
	Other powers 
	                     1. Original, exclusive and final authority to dispute settlement regarding president, vice president election.
                     2. Enquire conduct, behaviour of chairman/members of UPSC on prez reference. SC Court advice is binding on the president.
                     3. Ultimate interpreter of the Constitution.
	

	Constitution Bench
	                     * Constitutional Bench (Article 145(3)): Formed by the CJI, consisting of at least 5 judges to decide substantial constitutional questions. It is formed when:
                     * A substantial question of law on Constitution arises.
                     * The President seeks SC’s opinion (Article 143).
                     * Conflicting decisions exist between 2 or more 3-judge benches.
                     * A later 3-judge bench finds an earlier 3-judge bench ruling doubtful. 
                     * Largest Bench: 13 judges (Keshavananda Bharati case, 1973).
                     * Division Bench: Comprises 2 or 3 judges, handling most cases.
                     * Regional benches of the Supreme Court of India can be established by the Chief Justice of India (CJI) with the prior approval of the President of India. Does Not need CAA.
	Article 145 (3)
	Supreme court advocates
	Senior Advocates:
                     * Designated by SC/HC with their consent for expertise and standing at the Bar (Advocates Act, 1961). 
                     * Senior Advocates primarily focus on arguing cases in court rather than handling procedural tasks. Thus, cannot file pleadings, draft affidavits, or appear without an AoR or junior advocate. But prohibition can't extend if done in consultation with a junior.
Advocate-on-Record (AoR):
                     * Only AoRs can file cases and documents in the SC.
                     * Must clear the SC's AoR exam. 
                     * Can file cases & documents, argue in the SC. 
Other Advocates:
                     * Enrolled with State Bar Council under Advocates Act, 1961.
                     * Can argue in SC, only if instructed by an AoR or permitted by the court. 
                     * cannot file documents or cases.
	

	

Indian Supreme Court
	American Supreme Court
	Narrow original jurisdiction- confined to federal cases.
	Wide original jurisdiction- federal cases + cases relating to naval forces, maritime activities, ambassadors, etc.
	Narrow JR- Procedure estd by law
	Wide JR -‘due process of law’.
	Wide appellate jurisdiction- constitutional, civil, and criminal cases.
	Narrow appellate jurisdiction- constitutional cases only.
	Art 136- Special Leave
	No.
	It has advisory jurisdiction.
	No
	Its jurisdiction and powers can be enlarged by Parliament.
	Limited jurisdiction & powers (only those conferred by Constitution)
	It has power of judicial superintendence and control over state high courts due to an integrated judicial system.
	It has no such power due to a double (or separated) judicial system.
	 
Issues in Judiciary:
                     * Appointments:
                     * Lack of transparency in the collegium system and “uncle judge” syndrome.
                     * Delays in appointments, often due to executive overreach.
                     * Solutions-
                     * The UK has a Judicial Appointments Commission with 15 members from the judiciary and legal professions, while South Africa's Judicial Service Commission (JSC) advises the President on appointments and includes top judges, the Justice Minister, legal professionals, and National Assembly representatives.
                     * Case Management:
                     * Backlog of cases (e.g., 80k pending in 2023).
                     * Judicial overreach vs activism (e.g., COVID-19).
                     * Master of the Roster:
                     * CJI’s administrative powers criticized for lack of transparency.
Way Forward:
                     * Case Management:
                     * E-filing, digital courts, and Supreme Court Reports.
                     * The 24×7 Open and Networked Courts in Kerala enhance judicial accessibility, efficiency, and transparency, reducing case backlog and ensuring round-the-clock legal assistance.
                     * Regional SC branches as suggested in the 229th Law Commission report.
	

Aspect
	Details
	Definition
	Quasi-judicial bodies established by parliament to handle specific disputes (Civil not criminal) outside the traditional court system.
	Introduced by
	42nd CAA, 1976- Part XIV- A
	Governing Articles
	Article 323A (Administrative Tribunals) 
Article 323B (Other Tribunals)
	Jurisdiction
	Tribunals restrict the jurisdiction of ordinary courts, leading to debates on judicial independence and Separation of powers.
	Appeals
	Dilutes system of appeal- Tribunals like NGT, NCLAT, SAT, AFT, and TDSAT allow direct appeals to the Supreme Court, bypassing High Courts
(SAT - securities; TD - telecom disputes settlement ) 
	Advantages
	                     * Swift justice- Ex: NGT – M.C. Mehta vs UOI (2017): Strengthened air pollution controls in Delhi-NCR.
                     * Simplified process- Follows principle of natural justice Ex: Consumer Disputes Redressal Commissions follow simplified online filing and disposal rules.
                     * Saving Cost & Time- Ex: Essar Steel Case (2019): NCLT resolved ₹54,000 crore debt in record time, ensuring faster recovery and reduced litigation costs.
                     * Lightens court load- Ex: CAT settled over 90% of service disputes of government employees, easing the burden on High Courts. 
                     * Expert driven decisions- Novartis vs UOI (2013): Rejected patent to prevent expensive cancer drug monopolies based on drug’s evergreening. 
	Challenges
	                     * Slow & Overloaded – Tribunals lack resources, causing delays.
Example: Vijay Mallya Loan Default Case (2017) – DRT struggled with a backlog, delaying bank recoveries.
                     * Separation of Powers – Executive controls tribunal appointments.
Example: Government vs NGT (2021) – Allegations of biased appointments in NGT affected independent decision-making.
                     * Opaque Process – Nirav Modi PNB Fraud Case (2018) – Lack of transparency in SEBI's regulatory actions, as noted in SAT's review, raised concerns about accountability. 
                     * Accessibility – Limited reach makes access difficult.
Ex: IPAB Closure (2021) – The shutdown of IPAB left patent cases pending, forcing litigants to approach High Courts. 
	Central Administrative Tribunal 
(article 323 A)
	Art 323 A empowers parliament to provide estd of admin tribunals for adjudication of disputes relating to recruitment and conditions of service of persons appointed to public service of centre, state, local bodies, public corp. 
Aim- Speedy and inexpensive justice.
                        * Established by parliament: 1985, Principal Bench in Delhi and 17 regular benches.
                        * Jurisdiction: Original jurisdiction covering All India Services, Central Civil Services, and civil posts under the center. 
                        * Excludes defense personnel, SC officers, and parliamentary staff.
                        * Composition: Chairman + members; appointed by President. Members are drawn from both judicial and admin streams on the basis of a high powered selection committee chaired by sitting SC judge + Approval of Appointment committee of cabinet. (No Vice chairman)
                        * Term: 5 years; retirement at 65 (Chairman), 62 (Members).
                        * Procedure: Not bound by Civil Procedure Code; follows natural justice.; Nominal fee is 50 rs.
                        * Appeals: Initially to SC, but L. Chandra Kumar case (1997) ruled appeals go to High Courts first.
	State administrative tribunal 
(Art 323 A)
	                        * Establishment: Set up under the 1985 Act on state request by Parliament; exists in 9 states 
                        * Appointment: President appoints Chairman & members after Governor's consultation.
                        * Original jurisdiction w.r.t state govt employees. 
The act also provides for JAT (Joint admin tribunal)
                        * There’s No hierarchy of tribunals in art 323 A as there's only 1 tribunal for centre, one for each state
	Tribunal for other matters (Article 323B)
	                        * Scope: Article 323B allows tribunals for foreign exchange- import-export, industrial disputes; land reforms; taxation; elections to parliament & state legislature, rent & tenancy rights(FIILTER) + Food.
                        * Authority: Both Parliament & state legislatures can establish them.
                        * There maybe a hierarchy of tribunals
                        * Prez, VP- art 71 to SC- Under the RPA, 1951, all election disputes involving MPs and MLAs must be filed as "election petitions" directly in the High Court of the respective state. While Article 323B of the Constitution empowers Parliament or state legislatures to establish specialized tribunals for election disputes, no such tribunal has been created for MP or MLA elections to date. Consequently, these disputes remain under the original jurisdiction of the High Courts, with a final right of appeal to the Supreme Court
	Way forward
	1️⃣ Increase Staff & Infrastructure –📖 Law Commission 272nd Report (2017) emphasized filling vacancies faster.
2️⃣ Ensure Transparency – Implement uniform procedures, structured guidelines, and open hearings to improve public trust. ⚖️ SC – L. Chandra Kumar (1997) called for periodic tribunal review to enhance efficiency.
3️⃣ Independent Appointments –📖 47th Law Commission Report (1972) suggested judicial-led appointments for fairness.
4️⃣ Expand Tribunal Reach 📖 Law Commission 74th Report (1978) proposed expanding tribunal presence beyond metro cities, ideally near HC.

 Bar council
                        * Statutory body under Advocates act 1961.
                        * Regulate legal practice & legal edu in india in consultation with univ & state bar councils. 
                        * Allows foreign lawyers, law firms to practice in non-litigious matters.
                        * Can't appear - court, tribunal, regulatory authority.
                        * Bar council of India- Chairman and vice chairman elected by council from amongst its members- hold office for a period of 2 years. 
                        * Meghalaya is 1st state to operationalise social audit law. 
HC (214- 231 in part 6)
Aspect
	Details
	Jurisdiction
	                        * Total 25 HCs and only 3 HCs have jurisdiction of more than one states
                        * 7th CAA: Parliament has power to establish a common HC for 2 or more than states. Example-  
                        * Bombay HC → MH, Goa, Dadra & Nagar Haveli, Daman & Diu.
                        * Guwahati HC → Assam, Nagaland, Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh. (AruNa AsMi)
                        * Punjab & Haryana HC → Punjab, Haryana, Chandigarh.
                        * Freedom to appoint their own staff. 
	Appointment 
(A. 217)
	                        * CJ of HC= appointed by President, after consultation with CJI & Governor.
                        * Other judges of HC- President after consultation CJI (+  2 senior most judges collegium) + CJ of HC + Governor
                        * Transfer of Judges= Prez + CJI (+ 4 senior most judges collegium) + CJs of 2 HCs concerned (3rd Judges case)
                        * Receives compensatory allowance decided by Parliament
                        * SC (1974): Judicial review needed to prevent arbitrariness. Only transferred judges can challenge their transfers if they are arbitrary.
                        * SC (1977): Transfers only in public interest, not as punishment.
                        * SC 1999 Opinion- CJI’s opinion means plurality of judges, ie, CJI must consult 4 senior SC judges. 
                        * Every HC consists of CJ & other judges as president (parliament in SC) may determine from time to time- Constitution leaves at the president's discretion the strength of HC. 
	Oath or affirmations (A. 219)
	                        * Administered by the Governor or a person appointed by the administrator. 
                        * Oath (same as SC) (FACISt)
                        * Perform duties faithfully & impartially. No influence from favor, affection, ill-will, or pressure.
                        * True and Allegiance to the Constitution of India 
                        * To uphold the constitution and & its laws
                        * Uphold sovereignty and integrity of India.
                        * Set out in the 3rd Schedule.
	Qualification
	                        * Advocate in HC for 10 years or
                        * judicial officer for 10 years
                        * If an advocate later becomes a judicial officer, both experiences are counted.
	Tenure
	                        * Retirement age: 62 years (Raised by 15th CAA, 1963)
                        * If age is disputed, the President (with CJI consultation) decision is final. 
                        * Vacates office when appointed in SC or transferred in another HC
                        * Resignation- Submits resignation in writing to the President.
	Removal (A. 217 & 218)
	                        * Judges Enquiry Act, 1968 regulates procedure- Impeachment motion can be initiated in either house of parliament (same process as SC removal)
                        * Removed by the President after Parliament’s address. 
                        * Conduct in Parliament- Cannot be discussed unless motion of removal is underway
	(A. 220)
	                        * Restrictions after being permanent Judge- Cannot plead/act in any court except SC & other HCs.
	Salary & Allowances (Article 221)
	                        * Salaries & Allowances of HC Judges → Consolidated Fund of the State
                        * Pension of Retired HC Judges → Consolidated Fund of India (Article 112)
                        * Admin Expenses of the High Court → charged n non votable.
                        * Salary & allowances Determined by Parliament, specified in 2nd schedule. Not changeable to their disadvantage after appointment.
                        * NOTE- Both Parliament & State legislature can increase jurisdiction and powers of HC.  (Its jurisdiction cannot be curtailed)
                        * Role of Parlia in HC- Establishing common HC + Extending HC jurisdiction to UTs + Increasing jurisdiction + deciding salaries + recommending removal (varun sir)
                        * Role of Prez- Determines strength of HC judge as per his discretion.
                        * Role Governor- Only in recommending appointments and NOT transfers.
                        * Role of state legislature- Only in increasing jurisdiction of HC
	Writ Jurisdiction on CJ HC
	                        * Art 226- While the Chief Justice holds exclusive authority over matters such as case allocation, staff appointments, and rule-making (under Article 229), this authority is not absolute. when the Chief Justice (CJ) of a High Court acts in an administrative capacity, their decisions are subject to the writ jurisdiction of other judges of the same High Court under Article 226
                        * In the Chandra Kumar case (1997), the SC ruled that the writ jurisdiction of the HCs and the SC is part of the basic structure of the Constitution. This means it cannot be removed or limited by any constitutional amendment
	Additional & Acting Judges
	                        * Acting CJ of HC- Prez appointed when Vacant, absent, inability. 
                        * Acting Judge (substitution):- Appointed by President (other than CJ) if a judge is unable to perform duties or is appointed as temporary CJ of that HC and holds office only until permanent judge takes charge. 
                        * Additional Judge (Workload):- Max tenure: 2 years. Appointed by the President for temporary increase in court business  or arrears in work.
                        * Retired Judge (Assistance)- art 224A-  By CJ of HC with consent of prez..
	Original Jurisdiction
	                        * Power to hear disputes in the first instance.
                        * Matters related to admiralty, 
                        * Revenue matters (not pre constitutional)
                        * Cases involving interpretation of constitution from lower courts 
                        * Constitutional interpretation- Both SC and HC can interpret constitution under their writ jurisdiction but SC’s interpretation is final and binding on all courts as per article 141.
                        * Note - interpretation word is mentioned in constitution for SC not HC
                        * Article 226 – writs for Fundamental Rights and other legal purposes.
                        * Election Disputes – over disputes related to MPs and MLAs.
                        * Contempt of Court (Art 215) – HCs can punish for contempt of themselves in subordinate courts. 
                        * Civil & Constitutional Cases – 4 HCs (Calcutta, Bombay, Madras, Delhi) handle high-value civil, matrimonial, and company law cases.
                        * Can a state challenge a central law?
                        * 2012 (MP vs. UOI): States cannot challenge Central laws under Article 131.
                        * 2015 (Jharkhand vs. Bihar): Took the opposite view, referred to a larger SC Bench for final decision
	Appellate jurisdiction 
	                        * Wider than original jurisdiction but narrower than appellate of SC 
                        * Civil matter-
                        * First appeal from order and judgment of District Court, Subordinate court on questions of law & fact (matter of right). 
                        * Second appeal on questions of law only (discretionary). 
                        * Calcutta, Bombay, Madras High Court provision for intra court appeal. If Single judge deciding case then appeal lies to division bench of same HC
                        * Appeal- decision- administrative tribunals to divisional bench of State HC.  1997.  Not possible for tribunals to go directly to SC sans going first to HC. 
                        * Criminal matter- if sentence is 
                        * Imprisonment is more than 7 years
                        * Death sentence is confirmed by the high court Whether appeal or not.. 
	Supervisory Jurisdiction (Admin + Judicial superintendence)
	                        * Exclusive of HC (absent in SC as it was never intended to supervise HC & subordinate courts . Hence SC has judicial superintendence & NOT supervisory jurisdiction) 
                        * Superintendence Power: Regulates courts/tribunal in territorial jurisdiction except military tribunals. 
                        * HC lays rules & laws for admin of justice at subordinate levels.
                        * To keep subordinate courts within bounds of their jurisdiction
                        * HC can act suo motu.
                        * Extraordinary Power and not an unlimited authority: Intervenes in jurisdictional excess, natural justice violations, legal errors, disregard of superior law, perverse findings, and manifest injustice.
	Control Over Subordinate Courts


	                        * Exclusive
                        * Administrative Superintendence: Governor consults HC for appointments, postings, promotions, dismissal (binding advice of high court) of district judges & appointments for judicial service. HC also oversees transfers, leave, and discipline of judicial officers.
                        * Withdraws cases pending in subordinate laws.  involving constitutional questions, either deciding or returning them.
                        * Judicial Superintendence- Binding Nature: Subordinate courts must follow High Court rulings.
NOTE - It has consultative powers not with governor but with respect to law & fact only 
	Court of Record 
	                        * Contempt of court is not defined in constitution
                        * As a court of record, it has power to correct its own judgement. But unlike SC (Art 137) it is not mentioned specifically in constitution
	JR
	                        * Legislative enactments & executive orders of Both CG & SG. 
	TRIVIA 
	SC is supreme b/c it is the highest court of appeal. But HC is not subordinate to SC
                        * Oldest HC - Calcutta, Bombay, Madras (CBM) , Allahabad(1866), Punjab (1875), karnataka (1884) - (APK)

                        * Assistant Sessions Judge

                        * Judicial services  exclusively refers to district judge & other inferior judicial post 
                        * Appointment of district judge
                        * No min age qualification in Constn
                        * Appointments, posting, Promotion= governor with high court consultation.
                        * Qualifications-
                        * Not in central, state service.
                        * Advocate pleader for 7 years.
                        * HC recommended.
                        * Appointment of other judge- Governor consulting with state public service commissions and HC.

                        * District judge, sessions judge
                        * Highest judicial authority in a district 
                        * Original, appellate jurisdiction in civil, criminal matter.
                        * District Judge = civil case- unlimited pecuniary powers
                        * Criminal case= session judge- unlimited- upto death sentence
                        * Judicial and administrative powers.
                        * Appeal against his order and Judgment lies to HC
                        * Power to impose any judgment - life imprisonment or capital punishment, but Capital Judgement, subjected to high court confirmation whether appeal or not

NALSA 1987
                        * Commonwealth human rights initiative (CHRI) report=  india= better per capita lawyer ratio= 1:736. But panel lawyers (NALSA)= 1 lawyers per 20,000
                        * Origin = Art 39A 
                        * Spirit = 14 & 22 (Equality before law, legal system for Justice on equality of opportunity) 
                        * 59th Law commission report = diff approaches for settlement before trial  (common for ADR )
                        * CJI = patron -in-chief of NALSA.
                        * Disburse funds -> NGO for legal awareness
                        * Functions
                        * Free, competent legal service
                        * Lok adalat - amicable dispute settlement
                        * Organise Legal awareness camp

                           * Free legal services
                           * payment of court, process fee. Charge payable or incurred with legal proceedings.
                           * Service of lawyer
                           * obtaining and supply of copy of order and documents in legal proceeding
                           * Preparation of Appeal Paper Book. Including printing and translation of documents.


                           * Eligible persons
                           * Women and children. 
                           * SC/ST members, NOT OBC
                           * Disabled persons
                           * Industrial workmen.
                           * Victim of mass disaster, Violence, drought, earthquake industrial disaster.
                           * Victim of trafficking, begar
                           * Person in custody.
                           * Annual Income limits
                           * Depend on limits by resp SG
                           * Gen less than 1 lakh ,
                           * Senior citizen less than 2 lakh
                           * Transgender less than 2 lakh
                           * Achievements
                           * Resolved cases > 1 crore ( judicial pendency )
                           * Benefits
                           * No fee. If paid = refunded
                           * Technicality free - Procedural flexibility
                           * Speedy trial  - Natural justice and not strict law application ( CrPC, evidence act )
                           * Binding award = decree. No appeal (hence not court of record)
                           * If not satisfied with the award, they can go to other courts. 
                           * Concerns
                           * Low skill set - 52% judicial officer rate
                           * Pvt lawyer fairly good
                           * Legal aid council = moderate
                           * Temporary empanelment => painstaking re-telling case history
                           * Better control- pvt lawyers
                           * No faith - last resort only
                           * Lok adalats
                           * 1st LA was in Gujarat 1982
                           * Statutory under LSAA 1987
                           * Based Gandhian principle
                           * Setting & compromise occurs at the pending and pre-litigation stage. 
                           * Pending case can- referred if-
                           * Both Parties agree or
                           * One party makes an application for referring. or
                           * Court satisfy- matter appropriate to be taken by Lok Adalat
                           * Composition
                           * Chairperson = sitting/ retd judicial OFFICER
                           * 2 members- 1 lawyer, 1 social worker 
                           * Alternate dispute redressal incl 
                           * Arbitration – A neutral third party (arbitrator) makes a binding decision like a private judge.
                           * Mediation – A mediator facilitates negotiation between parties to reach a mutual agreement.
                           * Conciliation – A conciliator actively suggests solutions to help parties resolve disputes amicably. Ex: Amicus Curiae
                           * Negotiation– Parties discuss & resolve disputes themselves without third-party intervention.
                           * Lok Adalat – A forum for quick, cost-effective dispute resolution with binding decisions, mainly for small cases. Lok Adalats: Have powers of a civil court under CPC but not CrPC. 
                           * Advantage - Alternate Viable, informal, cheap, expeditious ( VICE )
                           * Powers of civil court
                           * Power to regulate own procedure 
                           * Summon, Enforce attendance of witness 
                           * Requisition - pub record/ doc from court/ office
                           * Receive evidence on affidavit
                           * Judicial proceedings are deemed within meaning of IPC, and every Lok adalat shall be deemed as Civil court for purpose of CrPC
                           * Matters taken =  civil & certain criminal
                           * Civil -matrimonial, family dispute, land acquisition, labour dispute, telephone bill, municipality , Bank recovery cases, pension cases, housing board, slum clearance cases, consumer grievances, electricity matter.
                           * Compoundable criminal offence
                           * Outside purview 
                           * Divorces 
                           * Criminal cases- Non compoundable offence ( charges not droppable if complainant withdraw ) 
                           * Permanent Lok Adalat (PLA) 
                           * Features- 
                           * Estb under LSA, amended 2002; only on pre litigation matters
                           * Ensures free & competent legal aid to weaker sections.
                           * Composition: Chaired by a District Judge/ Additional District Judge or person ranked higher than district judge and person with judicial experience equivalent or superior to that of a District Judge & an expert in public utility services.
                           * Jurisdiction: Public utility disputes – transport, postal, telecom, power, sanitation, hospitals, etc.
                           * Pecuniary Limit: Initially ₹10 lakh, raised to ₹1 crore by Central Govt 
                           * No jurisdiction over non-compoundable offences.
                           * If settlement is possible, PLA formulates terms; if parties agree, it's passed; else, the dispute is decided on merit ( whereas in Lok adalats, if settlement is not reached the case is returned to court or parties are advised to seek remedy in court of law.
                           * Awards are final & binding
                           * (PLAs) and (LDs) are established by the State Legal Services Authorities (SLSAs) under the LSAA, 1987.
Family court
                           * Family court act 1984 = estb for conciliation, speedy settlement of marriage & family affair. 
                           * Features
                           * Estb by State govt, consult HC
                           * Obligatory - population more than 10 lakh (Metropolitan), other area deemed necessary (Note- Mega city= 100 lac)
                           * Matter in jurisdiction
                           * Matrimonial related 
                           * Property of spouse
                           * Declare legitimacy of a person
                           * Guardianship of person, custody of minor.
                           * Simple rule of evidence , procedure
                           * RT appeal => court of record, appeal in High court as per Family court act 1984
                           * Obj & reason- 
                           * Specialist court- deal exclusively with family matters with necessary expertise.
                           * Institutional mech for family dispute conciliation. 
                           * Inexpensive remedy provide 
                           * Flexibility & informal atmosphere
 
Fast track courts
                           * Estb 2000, by state govt
                           * Judges = ad-hoc basis , by HC 
                           * Aim= clear long pending
                           * 11th FC = recom.  estd 1734 fast track courts
                           * Problems
                           * Large money = create addnl post, little to ID prevalent, systematic issue
                           * More judges sans mech optimise = unintended result
                           * Min of law & justice
                           * 56% states don’t have
                           * SC data => 2010-17 = 1993 contradict more judges => resolution
                           * NLU study - state variation - case handled
 
Gram nyayalayas
                           * Formed under Grama Nyayalayas act 2008 by state govts - 114th law commission report
                           * Ensure non denial of justice due to socio-eco disability
                           * Judicial Magistrate 1st Class presides as Nyayadhikari (appointed by SG in consultation with HC).
                           * Established at intermediate panchayat level, Headquarter-based but mobile.
                           * Gram Nyayalayas handle civil disputes and compoundable criminal cases of minor nature (e.g., petty theft, simple hurt, labour disputes, and minor property issues), as notified by state governments.
                           * Follows summary procedure, conciliation first, local activists as conciliators
                           * Appeals:
                           * Criminal cases → Sessions Court (disposed within 6 months).
                           * Civil cases → District Court (disposed within 6 month
                           * Central Government (CG) and State Government (SG) have the power to amend the First and Second Schedules of the Grama Nyayalayas Act, 2008 which deals with civil & criminal 
                           * Former CJI Ramanna for an overwhelming majority of litigants the real and existing justice is only district judiciary & trial court. Without robust justice delivery at grassroot level, we can't imagine a healthy judiciary.
Legislature- Parliament (Art 79- 122 in part V)
                           * Parliament = supreme bicameral legislative body of Republic of India. It consists of 3 parts- RS, LS, President.
                           * President is an integral part of parliament but not a member of any house, contrary to American presidents. 
                           * British model
                           * House of lords ( RS - upper house )
                           * House of commons ( LS- lower house )
                           * Indian model is based on this 
                           * In 1954 hindi names rajya sabha & lok sabha were adopted in consti
                           * American model- Separation of Power
                           * Senate = upper house
                           * House of representatives = lower house
                           * Unicameral (consists president/ governor & Legislative only)
                           * Bicameral (ind based on this model)
                           * Ensures double reconsideration, preventing hasty decisions.
                           * Represents all sections of a large population and geographical regions.
                           * States can choose unicameral or bicameral legislatures.:
                           * Bicameral MAKTUB 
                           * Rajya sabha
                           * Also called= council of state , 2nd chamber , house of elders. 
                           * Provision of  Indirect election in indian polity intro by RS
                           * Composition - The “strength” of Lok Sabha (Article 81) and Rajya Sabha (Article 80) is mentioned in the Constitution, but the exact numbers can be altered by law.
                           * Max strength is fixed at 250 members. 238 - reps of states and UTS ; 12 are nominated by Prez.  229 (from states) 
4 (from UTs) 
RS 
245 members 
233 (States and UTs) - 
elected indirectly 
12 (Nominated by 
President)  
                           * States with highest RS seats - UP(31) →  Maharashtra (19) → Tamil Nadu (18) → Bihar= West Bengal (16) → Karnataka (12) - 
                           * SC (2018 judgement) - no NOTA applicable in RS elections
                              * Institutional mechanism to represent states. 
                              * 4th schedule- allocates seats in the Rajya Sabha to each state and union territory based on their population.
                              * State representation = indirectly elected State legislative assembly  member based on proportional rep by single transferable vote = South Africa se adopted
                              * Seat allocation = population basis (not sole basis, some small states given due weightage). Thus, Number of representatives varies from state to state.
                              * US = equal state rep ( symmetrical representation )
                              * UT with legislature=  Indirect election by Electoral College specifically constituted. DL, Puducherry, JK; other too small (PuDeJa)
                              * The Union Territories (UTs) that are not part of Article 240 of the Indian Constitution—meaning the President cannot directly make regulations for them under this specific article—are Delhi (NCT), Jammu & Kashmir, and Chandigarh. 
                              * Nominated members- Art 80- Prez = 12 members nominate. Based on their special knowledge or practical experience in the fields of: Literature, Art, Science, Social Service. 
                              * Term = fixed at 6 year
                              * Not fixed by constn, instead parliament under RPA 1951 fixed. Further act authorises president to make provision to govern order of retirement of RS members
                              * All not terminated @ same time. Thus when Lok Sabha is dissolved, Rajya Sabha can be called for imp discussion.
                              * Permanent body not subjected to dissolution. 1/3rd member retire every second year. 
                              * Open ballot for elections- via the Representation of the People (Amendment) Act, 2003 to curb cross-voting and horse trading (maintaining party discipline)
                              * Note-
                              * RS→ Seats are allocated to states based on population (not strictly proportional).
                              * LS → Seats are allocated to states based on population, and within each state, territorial constituencies are also divided based on population to ensure fair representation.
                              * Lok Sabha (house of ppl, 1st chamber, popular house)
                              * Art 81 = composition as specified and max strength fixed at 550 by constitution. 
                              * Max 530 state reps, max 20 UT (At present 543 total members)
                              * 104th CAA = removed Anglo indian ( Parliament + SLA )
                              * States with highest seats in LS -
                              * UP(80) > MH(48)> WB(42)> BR(40)> TN(39)> MP (29)> Karnataka (28)
                              * Term = art 83 fixed- 5 years. 
                              * 42 CAA changed -> 6 years, undone - 44 CAA
                              * Not continuing chamber. 
                              * 5 years count from the date of 1st meeting, after which it automatically dissolves. 
                              * The President can dissolve anytime before 5 years. This can't be challenged in a court of law.
                              * Further term can be extended during national emergency by parliament law for 1 year at a time for any length of time. But this can't be extended beyond 6 months after an emergency ceases to exist. 
                              * Reps= direct election 
                              * 61st CAA - reduced voting age ( from 21  to 18 )
                              * UT= “Direct election to house of ppl act” enables members to be chosen by direct election.
                              * Territorial constituencies
                              * Equal Seat-to-Population Ratio (Mostly): Lok Sabha seats are allotted based on population, but states with less than 60 lakh people get extra representation.
                              * Equal Constituency-to-Population Ratio (Within a State): All constituencies in a state have roughly the same population, no matter the area.
                              * Variation Between States: The ratio differs between states because smaller states get a minimum number of seats.
                              * Readjustment after each census
                              * Delimitation commission is established by Parliament act but appointed by President. It is responsible for redrawing the boundaries of territorial constituencies and total no of seats based on the latest Census.
                              * Parliament is empowered to determine the authority and manner in which delimitation is to be made. Thus it enacted delimitation commission acts 4 times- 1952, 1962, 1972 and 2002
                              * 42 CAA froze seats & territorial constituencies till 2000 at 1971 levels
                              * The 84th CAA extended this ban till 2026. 
                              * The 87th CAA changed levels to 2001 from 1971. 
                              * Limit on max member directly chosen may exceed if such increase is incidental to reorg of state by parliament act.
                              * Presently, it can be changed only after publishing relevant figures of the first census taken after 2026.
                              * Lok Sabha’s Superiority Over Rajya Sabha
                              * Joint Sitting– India has had three joint sittings (1961, 1977, 2002), and all three bills were passed due to Lok Sabha's majority.
                              * Money Bill– (Aadhaar Act, 2016; GST Bill (2016) Passed as a Money Bill, ignoring Rajya Sabha's amendments.
                              * LS support is compulsory for Govt/ PM Formation– (Manmohan Singh, 2004)- Despite being an RS MP, he became PM
                              * No-Confidence Motion– (Vajpayee, 1999) Lost by one vote in LS, forcing resignation despite RS strength.
                              * Parliament legislate on state laws under following conditions
                              * Art 249- RS = resolution - 2/3 present & voting
                              * Such law remains In force for 1 year. Expires after 6 months after resolution lapse. Hence minimum duration of such law = 1 year, max (without renewal) is 1.5 years
                              * If there’s any inconsistency (central, state laws) then central law would prevail
                              * Article 252- More than or 2 SLA resolution requesting
                              * Applicable- only where passed
                              * Power to amend/ repeal by parliament and not state
                              * Ex- Prize competition act 1955
                              * National emergency- become Inoperative after 6 months.
                              * President Rule- 
                              * Operative indefinitely
                              * Repealed/ Alter/ re-enact by the state legislature. 
                              * To implement Intl treaties/ conventions
                              * Ex - UN privileges & immunities act 1947
                              * Comparison RS position
                              * Equal status as LS 
                              * Ordinary bills- Intro, passage bills
                              * CAA
                              * Election & impeachment of prez, VP
                              * RS Alone only can initiate the procedure for VP removal.
                              * Remove CJI, CJ HC, CEC, CAG
                              * Approve emergency, ordinances
                              * Minister selection
                              * Jurisdiction enlargement SC, UPSC
                              * Note- The Parliament of India is the primary authority authorized to enlarge, extend, or restrict the jurisdiction (both territorial and subject matter) of a High Court. State Legislatures can modify the jurisdiction of a High Court only in specific matters allowed by the Constitution, but they cannot alter the territorial jurisdiction of a High Court.
                              * Supremacy of LS
                              * 108- Joint sitting
                              * 110- Money/ finance bill- intro and passage
                              * 112- Budget and demand for grants- voting 
                              * 117 (1) - Introduction of financial bill (1) 
                              * 352- Resolution for discontinuance of national Emergency 
                              * Passing of NCM (But RS can discuss & criticise policies of govt)
                              * Supremacy of RS
                              * A. 67 - initiation of motion of removal of Vice president - only in RS
                              * A. 249- Protect state power- 
                              * Empowers Parliament to legislate on a State List subject if RS passes a resolution with a 2/3rd majority, valid for one year (extendable).
